1.5 Calculus (Single Variable)
   1.5.1 Limits and Continuity
      1.5.1.1 Evaluate limits using direct substitution
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
==============================================================================
      LEARNING OBJECTIVE: 1.5.1.1 Evaluate limits using direct substitution
      ==============================================================================
      Context: 1.5 Calculus (Single Variable) > 1.5.1 Limits and Continuity
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      Difficulty profile: easy→medium mixed
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      Problem 1 (Infinite limit with direct substitution):
      Find the limit as x approaches 3 of the function f(x) = (x^2 - 9)/(x - 3).
      A) -6
      B) 0
      C) ∞
      D) Does not exist
      Correct Answer: C
      
      Solution:
      f(x) = (x+3)(x-3)/(x-3) = x+3. As x approaches 3, f(x) approaches 3+3 = 6. Since the limit becomes unbounded as x gets closer to 3, the limit is infinity.
      
      Explanation:
      Direct substitution shows that the function simplifies to x+3, and since x approaches 3, the limit becomes unbounded.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake would be to cancel out (x-3) in the numerator and denominator, which is not allowed when x = 3.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 2 (Zero in the denominator):
      Evaluate the limit as x approaches 0 of the function g(x) = (2x^2 + x)/x.
      A) 0
      B) 1
      C) 2
      D) Does not exist
      Correct Answer: D
      
      Solution:
      The function simplifies to g(x) = 2x + 1. As x approaches 0, the limit becomes 2(0) + 1 = 1. However, since there is a hole at x=0 (due to division by zero), the limit does not exist.
      
      Explanation:
      Direct substitution shows that the function simplifies to 2x+1, but there's a discontinuity at x=0 due to division by zero.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake would be to cancel out x in the numerator and denominator, which is only valid if x≠0. Since the limit involves x approaching 0, this method leads to an incorrect conclusion.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 3 (Polynomial with removable discontinuity):
      Find the limit as x approaches -2 of the function h(x) = (x^3 + 8)/(x+2).
      A) -16
      B) 0
      C) 4
      D) Does not exist
      Correct Answer: A
      
      Solution:
      The function can be factored as h(x) = (x+2)(x^2 - 2x + 4)/(x+2), which simplifies to x^2 - 2x + 4 when x≠-2. As x approaches -2, the limit becomes (-2)^2 - 2(-2) + 4 = 16.
      
      Explanation:
      Direct substitution after factoring shows that there's a removable discontinuity at x=-2, and the limit is found by evaluating the simplified function at x=-2.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake would be to cancel out (x+2) in the numerator and denominator without considering the discontinuity at x=-2. This leads to an incorrect conclusion.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 4 (Quotient of linear functions):
      Evaluate the limit as x approaches 1 of the function j(x) = (3x - 2)/(5x - 4).
      A) 0.6
      B) 0.8
      C) 1
      D) Does not exist
      Correct Answer: B
      
      Solution:
      The limit is found by direct substitution: j(1) = (3(1) - 2)/(5(1) - 4) = 1/1 = 1. However, the limit exists even though the function is undefined at x=1.
      
      Explanation:
      Direct substitution shows that the limit is 1, and it's important to remember that limits can exist even when the function is undefined at a specific point.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake would be to think that because the function is undefined at x=1, the limit does not exist. This is incorrect; the limit can still be found by evaluating the function as x approaches 1.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 5 (Rational function with a hole):
      Find the limit as x approaches 2 of the function k(x) = (x^2 - 4)/(x - 2).
      A) 0
      B) 2
      C) 4
      D) Does not exist
      Correct Answer: C
      
      Solution:
      The function can be factored as k(x) = (x-2)(x+2)/(x-2), which simplifies to x+2 when x≠2. As x approaches 2, the limit becomes 2+2 = 4.
      
      Explanation:
      Direct substitution after factoring shows that there's a removable discontinuity at x=2, and the limit is found by evaluating the simplified function at x=2.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake would be to cancel out (x-2) in the numerator and denominator without considering the discontinuity at x=2. This leads to an incorrect conclusion.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 6 (Limit of a constant):
      Find the limit as x approaches 4 of the function l(x) = 7.
      A) 0
      B) 3
      C) 7
      D) 10
      Correct Answer: C
      
      Solution:
      The limit is found by direct substitution: l(4) = 7. Since the function is constant, its value does not change as x approaches any point.
      
      Explanation:
      Direct substitution shows that the limit is equal to the constant value of the function, regardless of the input value.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake would be to think that the limit depends on the behavior of x as it approaches 4. However, since the function is a constant, its limit is simply the value of the function.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 7 (Limit of a trigonometric function):
      Find the limit as x approaches 0 of the function m(x) = sin(x)/x.
      A) 0
      B) 1
      C) π/2
      D) Does not exist
      Correct Answer: B
      
      Solution:
      The limit is found using L'Hopital's rule or by recognizing the limit as x approaches 0 of sin(x)/x is equal to the derivative of sine evaluated at 0, which is cos(0) = 1.
      
      Explanation:
      Direct substitution does not work here because it leads to an indeterminate form (0/0). However, the limit can be found using other techniques like L'Hopital's rule or recognizing a known limit.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake would be to attempt direct substitution and conclude that the limit does not exist. This is incorrect; the limit exists and can be found using alternative methods.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      1.5.1.2 Evaluate limits at infinity
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
==============================================================================
      LEARNING OBJECTIVE: 1.5.1.2 Evaluate limits at infinity
      ==============================================================================
      Context: 1.5 Calculus (Single Variable) > 1.5.1 Limits and Continuity
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      Difficulty profile: easy→medium mixed
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      Problem 1 (InfinityQuestion1):
      What is the limit as x approaches infinity of the function f(x) = 3x^2 + 5x - 7?
      A) -∞
      B) 0
      C) 5
      D) +∞
      Correct Answer: D
      
      Solution:
      As x approaches infinity, the term with the highest degree (3x^2) dominates the others. The limit is +∞.
      
      Explanation:
      The limit of a polynomial function as x approaches infinity is determined by the coefficient and exponent of the term with the highest degree. Since the highest degree term here is 3x^2 with a positive coefficient, the limit goes to +∞.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake would be to focus on the lower-degree terms (5x and -7) instead of the dominant term (3x^2). This would lead to incorrect conclusions about the limit.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 2 (InfinityQuestion2):
      Evaluate the limit as x approaches infinity for the function g(x) = (4x + 1)/(2x - 3)
      A) -∞
      B) 0
      C) 1/2
      D) +∞
      Correct Answer: D
      
      Solution:
      The limit is +∞ because the degrees of both numerator and denominator are equal (1), but the coefficient of the numerator's term is greater.
      
      Explanation:
      When comparing the degree of the numerator and denominator, if they're equal, the limit will be determined by their leading coefficients. Here, the leading coefficient in the numerator (4) is greater than that in the denominator (2), so the limit goes to +∞.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake would be to cancel out x from both terms, thinking they have the same degree. However, this is not allowed when evaluating limits at infinity.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 3 (InfinityQuestion3):
      Find the limit as x approaches infinity for h(x) = (2x^3 - 5x^2 + 7)/(3x^3 + 4)
      A) -∞
      B) 0
      C) 1/3
      D) +∞
      Correct Answer: D
      
      Solution:
      The limit is +∞ because the degree of the numerator (3) is equal to the degree of the denominator (3), but the coefficient of the numerator's term is greater.
      
      Explanation:
      When both the numerator and denominator have the same highest degree, the limit will be determined by their leading coefficients. In this case, the leading coefficient in the numerator (2) is greater than that in the denominator (3), so the limit goes to +∞.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake would be to cancel out x^3 terms without considering the coefficients. This is incorrect because it disregards the actual values of the leading terms.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 4 (InfinityQuestion4):
      Evaluate the limit as x approaches negative infinity for the function j(x) = (x^2 + 5)/(x - 1)
      A) -∞
      B) 0
      C) 1
      D) +∞
      Correct Answer: A
      
      Solution:
      The limit is -∞ because the degree of the numerator (2) is greater than the degree of the denominator (1), and the coefficient of x in the numerator is negative.
      
      Explanation:
      When the degree of the numerator is greater than the degree of the denominator, the limit will be either +∞ or -∞ depending on the signs of the leading coefficients. Here, since the leading term in the numerator (x^2) has a positive coefficient and x is approaching negative infinity, the overall sign is negative, resulting in a limit of -∞.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake would be to focus only on the degree difference without considering the signs of the coefficients. This can lead to an incorrect conclusion about the direction of the limit.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 5 (InfinityQuestion5):
      Find the limit as x approaches infinity for k(x) = (4x^2 - 3)/(7x^2 + 9)
      A) -∞
      B) 0
      C) 4/7
      D) +∞
      Correct Answer: C
      
      Solution:
      The limit is 4/7 because the degrees of both numerator and denominator are equal (2), and we can divide each term by x^2.
      
      Explanation:
      When both the numerator and denominator have the same highest degree, we can divide each term by the highest power of x to find the limit. Here, dividing by x^2 gives us 4/7 as the limit.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake would be to cancel out x^2 terms without considering the coefficients. This is incorrect because it disregards the actual values of the leading terms.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 6 (InfinityQuestion6):

      A) -∞
      B) 0
      C) 1/3
      D) +∞
      Correct Answer: A
      
      Solution:
      The limit is -∞ because the degree of the numerator (3) is equal to the degree of the denominator (3), and the coefficient of x^3 in the numerator is negative.
      
      Explanation:
      When both the numerator and denominator have the same highest degree, the limit will be determined by their leading coefficients. In this case, since the leading term in the numerator (x^3) has a negative coefficient and x is approaching negative infinity, the overall sign is negative, resulting in a limit of -∞.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake would be to cancel out x^3 terms without considering the coefficients. This is incorrect because it disregards the actual values of the leading terms.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 7 (InfinityQuestion7):
      Find the limit as x approaches infinity for m(x) = (x^4 - 2x^2 + 1)/(5x^4 + 3)
      A) -∞
      B) 0
      C) 1/5
      D) +∞
      Correct Answer: C
      
      Solution:
      The limit is 1/5 because the degrees of both numerator and denominator are equal (4), and we can divide each term by x^4.
      
      Explanation:
      When both the numerator and denominator have the same highest degree, we can divide each term by the highest power of x to find the limit. Here, dividing by x^4 gives us 1/5 as the limit.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake would be to cancel out x^4 terms without considering the coefficients. This is incorrect because it disregards the actual values of the leading terms.

      1.5.1.3 Evaluate one-sided limits
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
==============================================================================
      LEARNING OBJECTIVE: 1.5.1.3 Evaluate one-sided limits
      ==============================================================================
      Context: 1.5 Calculus (Single Variable) > 1.5.1 Limits and Continuity
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      Difficulty profile: easy→medium mixed
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      Problem 1 ([1.5.1.3]Q1):
      Find the limit as x approaches 2 from the left: \(\lim_{{x  \to 2^-}}  (x^2  - 4)\)
      A) -2
      B) 0
      C) 2
      D) Does not exist
      Correct Answer: D
      
      Solution:
      The limit does not exist because the function approaches different values as x approaches 2 from the left and right. From the left, it approaches -2 (since \((x-2)(x+2)\) becomes \((-1)(4) = -4\)), while from the right it approaches 4 (as x gets closer to 2 from above).
      
      Explanation:
      One- sided limits are evaluated by considering only values of x approaching a particular point from one direction. In this case, we look at values less than 2.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake is to assume that the limit exists because the function value at x=2 is well-defined. However, for one-sided limits, it's the behavior of the function near the point, not the value at the point itself, that matters.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 2 ([1.5.1.3]Q2):
      Evaluate \(\lim_{{x  \to 0^+}}  \frac{1}{x}\).
      A) -∞
      B) 0
      C) 1
      D) +∞
      Correct Answer: D
      
      Solution:
      The limit is +∞ because as x approaches 0 from the right, the fraction becomes very large since the denominator gets closer to zero without reaching it.
      
      Explanation:
      When evaluating a one-sided limit approaching from the positive side of zero, we consider only values greater than zero. The function grows without bound in this case.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake is to think that the limit might be 0 because the function approaches 0 as x approaches 0 from both sides, but this is not a one-sided limit.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 3 ([1.5.1.3]Q3):
      Find \(\lim_{{x  → -∞}}  (2x + 5)\).
      A) -∞
      B) 0
      C) 5
      D) +∞
      Correct Answer: A
      
      Solution:
      The limit is -∞ because as x approaches negative infinity, the term 2x becomes more and more negative, causing the entire expression to become arbitrarily large in magnitude.
      
      Explanation:
      When evaluating a limit as x approaches negative infinity, we consider values of x that are increasingly negative. The linear function grows without bound in this case, but in the negative direction.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake is to think that the limit might be 5 because it's the constant term in the expression, but the dominant term is 2x which drives the limit.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 4 ([1.5.1.3]Q4):
      Evaluate \(\lim_{{x  → 0^-}}  \frac{\sin(x)}{x}\).
      A) -1
      B) 0
      C) 1
      D) Does not exist
      Correct Answer: C
      
      Solution:
      The limit is 1, which can be proven using L'Hopital's rule or by recognizing that the function approaches the slope of the tangent line to y=sin(x) at x=0.
      
      Explanation:
      This limit is a special case known as the squeeze theorem limit. As x approaches 0 from the left, sin(x)/x approaches the value of the sine function divided by its input, which is 1 at x=0.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake is to think that the limit might be 0 because both numerator and denominator approach 0, but this is not a one-sided limit. L'Hopital's rule or the squeeze theorem must be applied.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 5 ([1.5.1.3]Q5):

      A) -9
      B) 0
      C) 9
      D) +∞
      Correct Answer: D
      
      Solution:
      The limit is +∞ because as x approaches 3 from the right, the denominator becomes very small (without reaching zero), causing the entire expression to become arbitrarily large.
      
      Explanation:
      This is a one-sided limit approaching from the positive side of 3. The function grows without bound as x gets closer to 3 from above.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake is to think that the limit might be 0 because the denominator approaches zero, but this is not a two-...
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 6 ([1.5.1.3]Q6):
      Evaluate \(\lim_{{x  → π/2^-}}  \tan(x)\).
      A) -∞
      B) 0
      C) π/2
      D) +∞
      Correct Answer: D
      
      Solution:
      The limit is +∞ because as x approaches π/2 from the left, tan(x) increases without bound since it's the ratio of sin(x) and cos(x), both approaching 1 but sin(x) grows faster than cos(x).
      
      Explanation:
      This is a one-sided limit approaching from the negative side of π/2. The tangent function approaches infinity as x gets closer to π/2 from below.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake is to think that the limit might be π/2 because it's the value at which the function becomes undefined, but this is not a one-sided limit.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 7 ([1.5.1.3]Q7):
      Find \(\lim_{{x  → 0}}  (\frac{1}{|x|})\).
      A) -1
      B) 0
      C) Does not exist
      D) +∞
      Correct Answer: C
      
      Solution:
      The limit does not exist because the function approaches different values as x approaches 0 from the left and right. From the left, it approaches +∞ (since |x| becomes very small), while from the right it also approaches +∞.
      
      Explanation:
      This is a two-sided limit, but since we're asked to return only one-sided limits, we can say that there's no one-sided limit as x approaches 0. The function behaves differently depending on which direction x comes from.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake is to think that the limit exists because the function seems continuous at x=0, but it's not a one-sided limit.

      1.5.1.4 Evaluate infinite limits
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
==============================================================================
      LEARNING OBJECTIVE: 1.5.1.4 Evaluate infinite limits
      ==============================================================================
      Context: 1.5 Calculus (Single Variable) > 1.5.1 Limits and Continuity
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      Difficulty profile: easy→medium mixed
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      Problem 1 (:question_1):
      What is the limit of the function f(x) = 2x^2 + x - 3 as x approaches infinity?
      A) -∞
      B) 0
      C) ∞
      D) 1
      Correct Answer: C
      
      Solution:
      As x approaches infinity, the highest power term (2x^2) dominates the function. Thus, the limit is ∞.
      
      Explanation:
      The limit of a polynomial with the highest degree term having a positive coefficient approaches infinity as x goes to infinity.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake would be to choose option A (-∞), which occurs when the highest degree term has a negative coefficient. Here, since the coefficient is positive, the limit is positive infinity.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 2 (question_2):
      Evaluate the limit of the function g(x) = (4x - 5)/(3x + 2) as x approaches infinity.
      A) -∞
      B) 0
      C) 1
      D) ∞
      Correct Answer: C
      
      Solution:
      The limit can be simplified by dividing both the numerator and denominator by the highest power of x (x in this case). This gives us (4 - 5/x)/(3 + 2/x), which approaches (4 - 0)/(3 + 0) = 1 as x approaches infinity.
      
      Explanation:
      When evaluating limits with rational functions, it's often helpful to divide both the numerator and denominator by the highest power of x present in either part.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake would be to cancel out the x terms without considering their coefficients, leading to an incorrect conclusion that the limit is 1/3. However, this approach overlooks the fact that the limit of (5/x) and (2/x) as x approaches infinity is zero.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 3 (question_3):
      Find the limit of h(x) = x^3 - 4x + 7 as x approaches negative infinity.
      A) -∞
      B) 0
      C) 1
      D) ∞
      Correct Answer: A
      
      Solution:
      As x approaches negative infinity, the highest power term (x^3) dominates the function with a positive coefficient. Thus, the limit is -∞.
      
      Explanation:
      The limit of a polynomial with the highest degree term having a negative coefficient approaches negative infinity as x goes to negative infinity.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake would be to choose option D (∞), which occurs when the highest degree term has a positive coefficient. Here, since the coefficient is negative, the limit is negative infinity.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 4 (question_4):
      Evaluate the limit of p(x) = (2x^3 - 5x^2 + 7)/(x^2 + 3) as x approaches infinity.
      A) -∞
      B) 0
      C) 1
      D) ∞
      Correct Answer: C
      
      Solution:
      Dividing both the numerator and denominator by x^2, we get (2 - 5/x + 7/x^2)/(1 + 3/x^2), which approaches (2 - 0 + 0)/(1 + 0) = 2 as x approaches infinity.
      
      Explanation:
      When evaluating limits with rational functions, it's crucial to divide both the numerator and denominator by the highest power of x present in either part.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake would be to cancel out all x terms, leading to an incorrect conclusion that the limit is 2. However, this approach overlooks the fact that the limit of (5/x) and (7/x^2) as x approaches infinity is zero.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 5 (question_5):
      Find the limit of q(x) = 3x - 4sin(x)/x as x approaches infinity.
      A) -∞
      B) 0
      C) 1
      D) ∞
      Correct Answer: C
      
      Solution:
      As x approaches infinity, sin(x)/x approaches 0. Thus, the limit is (3x - 0)/(x) = 3, which approaches ∞ as x approaches infinity.
      
      Explanation:
      The limit of sin(x)/x approaches 0 as x goes to infinity, so the dominant term in the numerator is 3x.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake would be to think that sin(x)/x approaches 1 as x approaches infinity. However, this is not true; it actually approaches 0.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 6 (question_6):
      Evaluate the limit of r(x) = (2e^x - 3)/(e^x + 1) as x approaches infinity.
      A) -∞
      B) 0
      C) 1
      D) ∞
      Correct Answer: C
      
      Solution:
      Dividing both the numerator and denominator by e^x, we get (2 - 3/e^x)/(1 + 1/e^x), which approaches (2 - 0)/(1 + 0) = 2 as x approaches infinity.
      
      Explanation:
      When evaluating limits with exponential functions, it's helpful to divide both the numerator and denominator by the same base raised to the power of x.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake would be to cancel out the e^x terms without considering their coefficients, leading to an incorrect conclusion that the limit is 1. However, this approach overlooks the fact that the limit of (3/e^x) as x approaches infinity is zero.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 7 (question_7):
      Find the limit of s(x) = (x - 2)^2/(x + 1) as x approaches negative infinity.
      A) -∞
      B) 0
      C) 1
      D) ∞
      Correct Answer: A
      
      Solution:
      As x approaches negative infinity, the highest power term (x^2) dominates the function with a positive coefficient. Thus, the limit is -∞.
      
      Explanation:
      The limit of a polynomial with the highest degree term having a negative coefficient approaches negative infinity as x goes to negative infinity.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake would be to choose option D (∞), which occurs when the highest degree term has a positive coefficient. Here, since the coefficient is negative, the limit is negative infinity.

      1.5.1.5 Apply limit laws and properties
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
==============================================================================
      LEARNING OBJECTIVE: 1.5.1.5 Apply limit laws and properties
      ==============================================================================
      Context: 1.5 Calculus (Single Variable) > 1.5.1 Limits and Continuity
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      Difficulty profile: easy→medium mixed
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      Problem 1 (Asdf1):
      Evaluate the limit: lim x → 2 (3x - 6)
      A) (3 	imes 2) - 6 = 0
      B) 3(2) - 6 = 0
      C) 3(x - 2) = 0
      D) Does not exist
      Correct Answer: B
      
      Solution:
      The limit is evaluated by plugging in the value x approaches. lim x → 2 (3x - 6) = 3(2) - 6 = 0.
      
      Explanation:
      This question tests understanding of evaluating a simple linear function's limit as x approaches a constant.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake is to forget to substitute x with the given value, resulting in option A. Another possible mistake is not recognizing that the limit exists for this polynomial function.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 2 (Asdf2):
      Evaluate the limit: lim x → ∞ (5x^2 + 3x - 7)
      A) ∞
      B) -7
      C) 0
      D) Does not exist
      Correct Answer: A
      
      Solution:
      As x approaches infinity, the highest power term dominates. lim x → ∞ (5x^2 + 3x - 7) = lim x → ∞ (5x^2) = ∞.
      
      Explanation:
      This question tests understanding of evaluating limits with polynomial functions where the degree of the leading term is greater than 1.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake is to focus on the lower-degree terms, like -7 or 3x, which become insignificant compared to the dominant x^2 term.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 3 (Asdf3):
      Evaluate the limit: lim x → 0 (sin(x) / x)
      A) 1
      B) 0
      C) -1
      D) Does not exist
      Correct Answer: A
      
      Solution:
      This is a famous limit that evaluates to 1, based on the limit definition of the derivative of sin(x) at x=0. lim x → 0 (sin(x) / x) = 1.
      
      Explanation:
      The question tests understanding of special limits related to trigonometric functions and their derivatives.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake is to assume that the limit is 0/0, which would require L'Hopital's rule. However, this specific limit has a known value.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 4 (Asdf4):
      Evaluate the limit: lim x → π (tan(x))
      A) ∞
      B) π
      C) 0
      D) Does not exist
      Correct Answer: D
      
      Solution:
      The tangent function is undefined at π, so the limit does not exist. lim x → π (tan(x)) = DNE.
      
      Explanation:
      This question tests understanding of evaluating limits at points where the function is undefined.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake is to assume that the limit exists and try to evaluate it, which would lead to incorrect conclusions.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 5 (Asdf5):
      Evaluate the limit: lim x → 1 (x^3 - 1) / (x - 1)
      A) 0
      B) 1
      C) 2
      D) 3
      Correct Answer: D
      
      Solution:
      The limit can be simplified using the difference of cubes formula: x^3 - 1 = (x - 1)(x^2 + x + 1). lim x → 1 (x^3 - 1) / (x - 1) = lim x → 1 (x^2 + x + 1) = 3.
      
      Explanation:
      The question tests understanding of applying limit laws to factor and simplify the function.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake is to forget to factor the numerator, leading to an indeterminate form or incorrect simplification.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 6 (Asdf6):
      Evaluate the limit: lim h → 0 (f(2+h) - f(2)) / h, if f(x) = x^2
      A) 4
      B) 8
      C) 16
      D) Does not exist
      Correct Answer: B
      
      Solution:
      The limit represents the derivative of f(x) at x=2. Using the power rule, f'(x) = 2x. So, f'(2) = 2 	imes 2 = 4. The limit evaluates to 8. lim h → 0 (f(2+h) - f(2)) / h = 8.
      
      Explanation:
      The question tests understanding of evaluating the derivative of a function using limits.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake is to apply the limit directly to f(x), without realizing it represents the derivative.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 7 (Asdf7):
      Evaluate the limit: lim x → 3 (x^2 - 9) / (x - 3)
      A) 0
      B) 3
      C) 6
      D) ∞
      Correct Answer: C
      
      Solution:
      The numerator can be factored as a difference of squares: x^2 - 9 = (x + 3)(x - 3). The limit evaluates to the coefficient of (x - 3), which is 6. lim x → 3 (x^2 - 9) / (x - 3) = 6.
      
      Explanation:
      The question tests understanding of applying limit laws to factor and simplify rational functions.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake is to cancel out the (x - 3) terms, which would be incorrect because it leads to division by zero.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      1.5.1.6 Evaluate indeterminate forms (0/0, ∞/∞)
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
==============================================================================
      LEARNING OBJECTIVE: 1.5.1.6 Evaluate indeterminate forms (0/0, ∞/∞)
      ==============================================================================
      Context: 1.5 Calculus (Single Variable) > 1.5.1 Limits and Continuity
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      Difficulty profile: easy→medium mixed
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      Problem 1 (Asymptote_0/0):
      Find the limit of the function f(x) = (x^2 - 4)/(x - 2) as x approaches 2.
      A) 0
      B) 1
      C) 2
      D) Does not exist
      Correct Answer: D
      
      Solution:
      The limit is of the form 0/0, which is indeterminate. To evaluate it, factor the numerator: (x - 2)(x + 2)/(x - 2). The (x - 2) term cancels out, leaving us with x + 2. As x approaches 2, the limit becomes 2 + 2 = 4. However, since there's a hole at x = 2, the limit does not exist.
      
      Explanation:
      The function is undefined at x = 2 because of the canceled factor, which creates a removable discontinuity or hole in the graph. Thus, the limit does not exist even though the algebraic manipulation gives us a finite value.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake is to cancel out (x - 2) without considering that it makes the function undefined at x = 2. This leads to the incorrect conclusion that the limit is 4.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 2 (Infinite_ Pole):
      Evaluate the limit of the function g(x) = (3x^2 + 6x)/(x^2 - 1) as x approaches ∞.
      A) 0
      B) 1
      C) 2
      D) ∞
      Correct Answer: D
      
      Solution:
      The limit is of the form ∞/∞, which is also indeterminate. To evaluate it, divide both numerator and denominator by x^2: (3 + 6/x)/(1 - 1/x^2). As x approaches infinity, the terms with x approach zero, leaving us with the limit of (3 + 0)/(1 - 0) = 3/1 = 3. However, since the function grows without bound as x increases, the limit is actually ∞.
      
      Explanation:
      The limit represents the behavior of the function as x becomes very large. Since both the numerator and denominator grow at the same rate (x^2), their ratio approaches infinity.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake is to cancel out x^2 without considering that this operation does not apply when working with infinity. The correct approach involves dividing by the highest power of x in both parts.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 3 (Factor_ and_Cancel):
      Find the limit of h(x) = (x^3 - 8)/(x - 2) as x approaches 2.
      A) 0
      B) 4
      C) 6
      D) ∞
      Correct Answer: C
      
      Solution:
      The limit is of the form 0/0, which is indeterminate. Factor the numerator: (x - 2)(x^2 + 2x + 4)/(x - 2). The (x - 2) term cancels out, leaving us with x^2 + 2x + 4. As x approaches 2, the limit becomes 2^2 + 2(2) + 4 = 12. Since 12/1 = 12, the limit is 12.
      
      Explanation:
      The function has a removable discontinuity at x = 2 due to the canceled factor. After canceling, we can evaluate the limit by plugging in x = 2 directly into the simplified expression.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake is to cancel out (x - 2) without considering that it makes the function undefined at x = 2. This leads to an incorrect conclusion about the limit.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 4 (L' Hopital's_Rule):
      Evaluate the limit of k(x) = sin(x)/x as x approaches 0.
      A) 0
      B) 1/2
      C) 1
      D) Does not exist
      Correct Answer: C
      
      Solution:
      The limit is of the form 0/0, which is indeterminate. Apply L'Hôpital's Rule by differentiating both numerator and denominator: cos(x)/1. As x approaches 0, cos(x) approaches 1 and 1 remains constant, so the limit becomes 1/1 = 1.
      
      Explanation:
      L'Hôpital's Rule is a technique for evaluating indeterminate forms like 0/0 or ∞/∞ by differentiating the numerator and denominator. In this case, it helps to resolve the indeterminacy.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake is to try to evaluate the limit without using L'Hôpital's Rule, which would result in an indeterminate form. Remember that L'Hôpital's Rule is specifically designed for these types of limits.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 5 (Polynomial_Division):
      Find the limit of m(x) = (x^3 - x)/(x - 1) as x approaches 1.
      A) 0
      B) 1
      C) 2
      D) 3
      Correct Answer: D
      
      Solution:
      The limit is of the form 0/0, which is indeterminate. Perform polynomial division: (x^2 + x + 1). As x approaches 1, the limit becomes 1^2 + 1 + 1 = 3.
      
      Explanation:
      Polynomial division allows us to rewrite the function as a sum of terms that do not have a common factor in the numerator and denominator. Once we've done this, we can evaluate the limit by plugging in x = 1.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake is to cancel out (x - 1) without considering that it makes the function undefined at x = 1. Instead, use polynomial division or factoring to simplify the expression.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 6 (Rationalization):
      Evaluate the limit of n(x) = sqrt(x + 4) - 2)/(x - 0) as x approaches 0.
      A) 0
      B) 1/2
      C) 1
      D) Does not exist
      Correct Answer: B
      
      Solution:
      The limit is of the form 0/0, which is indeterminate. Rationalize the numerator by multiplying both top and bottom by the conjugate: (sqrt(x + 4) - 2)(sqrt(x + 4) + 2)/(x). Simplify to get x/x = 1.
      
      Explanation:
      Rationalization involves multiplying the expression by a form that eliminates the square root. In this case, we multiply by the conjugate of the numerator to simplify the expression and resolve the indeterminacy.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake is to try to cancel out x without considering that it makes the function undefined at x = 0. Rationalization helps avoid this issue.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 7 (Infinite_Slope):
      Find the limit of p(x) = (x^2 - 1)/(x - 1) as x approaches 1.
      A) 0
      B) 1
      C) 2
      D) ∞
      Correct Answer: C
      
      Solution:
      The limit is of the form 0/0, which is indeterminate. Factor the numerator: (x - 1)(x + 1)/(x - 1). The (x - 1) term cancels out, leaving us with x + 1. As x approaches 1, the limit becomes 1 + 1 = 2.
      
      Explanation:
      The function has a removable discontinuity at x = 1 due to the canceled factor. After canceling, we can evaluate the limit by plugging in x = 1 directly into the simplified expression.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake is to cancel out (x - 1) without considering that it makes the function undefined at x = 1. This leads to an incorrect conclusion about the limit.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      1.5.1.7 Apply L'Hôpital's rule
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
==============================================================================
      LEARNING OBJECTIVE: 1.5.1.7 Apply L'Hôpital's rule
      ==============================================================================
      Context: 1.5 Calculus (Single Variable) > 1.5.1 Limits and Continuity
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      Difficulty profile: easy→medium mixed
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      Problem 1 (:1):
      Evaluate the limit as x approaches 0 for the function: (x^2 - 4)/(x - 2)
      A) (0)^2 - 4 = -4
      B) (0) - 2 = -2
      C) lim(x->0)[(2x)/(1)] = 0
      D) lim(x->0)[(2x)/(1)] = 0
      Correct Answer: D
      
      Solution:
      L'Hôpital's rule is applicable because the limit results in the indeterminate form 0/0. Differentiating both the numerator and denominator, we get (2x)/(1). Evaluating this at x=0 gives us 0.
      
      Explanation:
      Choice A is incorrect because it evaluates the function at x=0 without applying L'Hôpital's rule. Choice B incorrectly applies the limit to the original function instead of differentiating.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake is to apply the limit directly to the original function without recognizing that L'Hôpital's rule is needed.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 2 (:2):
      Evaluate the limit as x approaches infinity for the function: (3x^2 + 5)/(7x^2 - 4)
      A) (3∞^2 + 5)/(7∞^2 - 4) = ∞/∞
      B) lim(x->∞)[(3/(7))] = 3/7
      C) lim(x->∞)[(6x)/(14x)] = 6/14
      D) lim(x->∞)[(3/(7))] = 0
      Correct Answer: B
      
      Solution:
      The limit results in the indeterminate form ∞/∞, so L'Hôpital's rule applies. Differentiating both numerator and denominator, we get (6x)/(14x), which simplifies to 3/7 as x approaches infinity.
      
      Explanation:
      Choice A correctly identifies the indeterminate form but does not apply L'Hôpital's rule. Choice C incorrectly simplifies the fraction before applying L'Hôpital's rule, while choice D incorrectly sets the limit to zero without considering the correct application of L'Hôpital's rule.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake is to simplify the function before applying L'Hôpital's rule or not recognizing that ∞/∞ is an indeterminate form.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 3 (:3):
      Evaluate the limit as x approaches 2 for the function: (x^3 - 8)/(x - 2)
      A) (2)^3 - 8 = 0
      B) (2) - 2 = 0
      C) lim(x->2)[(3x^2)/(1)] = 12
      D) lim(x->2)[(3x^2)/(1)] = 6
      Correct Answer: C
      
      Solution:
      The limit results in the indeterminate form 0/0, so L'Hôpital's rule applies. Differentiating both numerator and denominator, we get (3x^2)/(1). Evaluating this at x=2 gives us 12.
      
      Explanation:
      Choice A evaluates the function at x=2 without applying L'Hôpital's rule. Choice B incorrectly applies the limit to the original function instead of differentiating. Choice D has a calculation error, as it should be 3x^2, not 6x^2.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake is to apply the limit directly to the original function or incorrect differentiation.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 4 (:4):
      Evaluate the limit as x approaches infinity for the function: (e^x - 1)/(x)
      A) (e^∞ - 1)/∞ = ∞/∞
      B) lim(x->∞)[(e^x)/(x)] = 0
      C) lim(x->∞)[(e^(x-1))/1] = ∞
      D) lim(x->∞)[(e^x)/(x)] = ∞
      Correct Answer: D
      
      Solution:
      The limit results in the indeterminate form ∞/∞, so L'Hôpital's rule applies. Differentiating both numerator and denominator, we get (e^x)/(1), which approaches infinity as x approaches infinity.
      
      Explanation:
      Choice A correctly identifies the indeterminate form but does not apply L'Hôpital's rule. Choice B incorrectly sets the limit to zero without considering the correct application of L'Hôpital's rule. Choice C simplifies the fraction before applying L'Hôpital's rule, which is incorrect.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake is to simplify the function or set the limit to an incorrect value without recognizing that ∞/∞ is an indeterminate form.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 5 (:5):
      Evaluate the limit as x approaches 0 for the function: (sin(x))/x
      A) (sin(0))/0 = 0/0
      B) lim(x->0)[(cos(x))/(1)] = 1
      C) lim(x->0)[(1/cos(x))]
      D) lim(x->0)[(cos(x))/(1)] = 0
      Correct Answer: B
      
      Solution:
      The limit results in the indeterminate form 0/0, so L'Hôpital's rule applies. Differentiating both numerator and denominator, we get (cos(x))/(1). Evaluating this at x=0 gives us 1.
      
      Explanation:
      Choice A correctly identifies the indeterminate form but does not apply L'Hôpital's rule. Choice C incorrectly simplifies the fraction before applying L'Hôpital's rule. Choice D has a calculation error, as it should be cos(x), not sin(x).
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake is to apply the limit directly to the original function or incorrect differentiation.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 6 (:6):
      Evaluate the limit as x approaches 1 for the function: (x^3 - 1)/(x - 1)
      A) (1)^3 - 1 = 0
      B) (1) - 1 = 0
      C) lim(x->1)[(3x^2)/(1)] = 3
      D) lim(x->1)[(3x^2)/(1)] = 0
      Correct Answer: C
      
      Solution:
      The limit results in the indeterminate form 0/0, so L'Hôpital's rule applies. Differentiating both numerator and denominator, we get (3x^2)/(1). Evaluating this at x=1 gives us 3.
      
      Explanation:
      Choice A evaluates the function at x=1 without applying L'Hôpital's rule. Choice B incorrectly applies the limit to the original function instead of differentiating. Choice D has a calculation error, as it should be 3x^2, not 0.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake is to apply the limit directly to the original function or incorrect differentiation.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 7 (:7):
      Evaluate the limit as x approaches infinity for the function: (ln(x))/x
      A) (ln(∞))/∞ = ∞/∞
      B) lim(x->∞)[(1/x)] = 0
      C) lim(x->∞)[(1/(e^x))] = 0
      D) lim(x->∞)[(1/x)] = ∞
      Correct Answer: B
      
      Solution:
      The limit results in the indeterminate form ∞/∞, so L'Hôpital's rule applies. Differentiating both numerator and denominator, we get (1)/(x), which approaches 0 as x approaches infinity.
      
      Explanation:
      Choice A correctly identifies the indeterminate form but does not apply L'Hôpital's rule. Choice C incorrectly simplifies the fraction before applying L'Hôpital's rule. Choice D sets the limit to an incorrect value without considering the correct application of L'Hôpital's rule.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake is to simplify the function or set the limit to an incorrect value without recognizing that ∞/∞ is an indeterminate form.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      1.5.1.8 Use squeeze theorem
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
==============================================================================
      LEARNING OBJECTIVE: 1.5.1.8 Use squeeze theorem
      ==============================================================================
      Context: 1.5 Calculus (Single Variable) > 1.5.1 Limits and Continuity
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      Difficulty profile: easy→medium mixed
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      Problem 1 (:1):
      Evaluate the limit using the Squeeze Theorem: lim x → ∞ (sin(x) / x)
      A) 0
      B) 1
      C) π/2
      D) ∞
      Correct Answer: A
      
      Solution:
      The function sin(x)/x oscillates between -1 and 1 as x approaches infinity. Since the absolute value of sin(x)/x is always less than or equal to 1, we can use the Squeeze Theorem. Thus, lim x → ∞ (sin(x) / x) = 0.
      
      Explanation:
      The Squeeze Theorem states that if a function f(x) is squeezed between two functions g(x) and h(x), where g(x) ≤ f(x) ≤ h(x) for all x in an interval containing a, then if the limits of g(x) and h(x) at a are equal, the limit of f(x) at a also exists and is equal to that limit.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake would be to assume that the limit is 1 because sin(x) oscillates between -1 and 1. However, the Squeeze Theorem requires that the function is squeezed _between_ two bounding functions, not just oscillating within a range.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 2 (:2):
      Evaluate the limit using the Squeeze Theorem: lim x → 0 (x / |x|)
      A) -1
      B) 0
      C) 1
      D) Does not exist
      Correct Answer: D
      
      Solution:
      The function x/|x| is undefined at x = 0, but we can analyze the limit as x approaches 0 from both sides. As x approaches 0 from the right (x > 0), the function equals 1; as x approaches 0 from the left (x < 0), it equals -1. Since the limit does not approach a single value, it does not exist.
      
      Explanation:
      The Squeeze Theorem cannot be applied here because there are no bounding functions that sandwich x/|x| for all x close to 0. The function is undefined at x = 0 and has different limits from the left and right.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake would be to assume that the limit exists based on the behavior of the function for non-zero values of x. However, the Squeeze Theorem requires continuity around the point in question, which is not the case here.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 3 (:3):
      Evaluate the limit using the Squeeze Theorem: lim x → 0 (tan(x) / x)
      A) 0
      B) π/2
      C) 1
      D) ∞
      Correct Answer: C
      
      Solution:
      The function tan(x)/x approaches 1 as x approaches 0. This is because tan(x) behaves like sin(x) and cos(x) behaves like 1 for small values of x, so tan(x)/x simplifies to sin(x)/x, which approaches 1 by the Squeeze Theorem.
      
      Explanation:
      The Squeeze Theorem can be applied here because tan(x)/x is well-defined near x = 0 and its behavior is similar to that of sin(x)/x. As x approaches 0, both sine and tangent approach zero, but cosine approaches 1, so the ratio approaches 1.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake would be to think that the limit is 0 because both tan(x) and x approach 0 as x approaches 0. However, this does not account for the fact that the ratio of these functions approaches a non-zero value.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 4 (:4):
      Evaluate the limit using the Squeeze Theorem: lim x → ∞ (e^x / x)
      A) 0
      B) 1
      C) ∞
      D) Does not exist
      Correct Answer: C
      
      Solution:
      The function e^x grows much faster than x as x approaches infinity. Therefore, the ratio e^x/x approaches infinity. However, we can use L'Hopital's Rule to confirm this result: lim x → ∞ (e^x / x) = lim x → ∞ (e^x / 1) = ∞.
      
      Explanation:
      The Squeeze Theorem is not applicable here because there are no bounding functions that sandwich e^x/x for all large values of x. However, the limit can be determined by comparing the rates of growth of e^x and x.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake would be to assume that the limit is 1 based on the intuition that 'large' numbers divided by 'very large' numbers approach zero. This reasoning does not account for the exponential growth of e^x.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 5 (:5):
      Evaluate the limit using the Squeeze Theorem: lim x → π (sin(x) / (x - π))
      A) -1
      B) 0
      C) π/2
      D) Does not exist
      Correct Answer: D
      
      Solution:
      The function sin(x)/(x - π) approaches ∞ as x approaches π from the left and approaches -∞ as x approaches π from the right. Since the limit does not approach a single value, it does not exist.
      
      Explanation:
      The Squeeze Theorem cannot be applied here because there are no bounding functions that sandwich sin(x)/(x - π) for all x close to π. The function has different limits from the left and right of π.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake would be to think that the limit exists based on the behavior of the function for non-π values of x. However, the Squeeze Theorem requires continuity around the point in question, which is not the case here.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 6 (:6):
      Evaluate the limit using the Squeeze Theorem: lim x → 0 (x^2 / sin(x))
      A) 0
      B) 1
      C) π/2
      D) ∞
      Correct Answer: B
      
      Solution:
      The function x^2/sin(x) approaches 1 as x approaches 0. This is because x^2 grows quadratically while sin(x) grows linearly, so the ratio approaches 1 as x approaches 0.
      
      Explanation:
      The Squeeze Theorem can be applied here because x^2/sin(x) is well-defined near x = 0 and its behavior is similar to that of x/x. As x approaches 0, both x^2 and sin(x) approach zero, but the ratio approaches 1.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake would be to think that the limit is 0 because both x^2 and sin(x) approach 0 as x approaches 0. However, this does not account for the fact that the ratio of these functions approaches a non-zero value.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 7 (:7):
      Evaluate the limit using the Squeeze Theorem: lim x → ∞ (sqrt(x^2 + 1) - x)
      A) 0
      B) 1
      C) π/2
      D) ∞
      Correct Answer: B
      
      Solution:
      The function sqrt(x^2 + 1) - x approaches 1 as x approaches infinity. We can use the difference of squares identity to rewrite the expression: (sqrt(x^2 + 1) - x)(sqrt(x^2 + 1) + x) = x^2 + 1 - x^2 = 1. Thus, sqrt(x^2 + 1) - x approaches 1.
      
      Explanation:
      The Squeeze Theorem can be applied here because the expression is well-defined for all positive values of x and its behavior can be analyzed using algebraic manipulation. As x grows large, the difference between sqrt(x^2 + 1) and x becomes negligible compared to x itself.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake would be to think that the limit is 0 because the square root function approaches x as x gets very large. However, this does not account for the fact that the expression involves a subtraction which causes it to approach a non-zero value.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      1.5.1.9 Evaluate limits with trigonometric functions
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
==============================================================================
      LEARNING OBJECTIVE: 1.5.1.9 Evaluate limits with trigonometric functions
      ==============================================================================
      Context: 1.5 Calculus (Single Variable) > 1.5.1 Limits and Continuity
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      Difficulty profile: easy→medium mixed
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      Problem 1 (:Q1):
      Find the limit: \(\lim_{{x  \to 0}}  \frac{\sin(x)}{x}\)
      A) \(-1\)
      B) \(0\)
      C) \(1\)
      D) \(+\infty\)
      Correct Answer: C
      
      Solution:
      The limit represents the derivative of sine at 0, which is equal to \(\cos(0) = 1\). Using L'Hopital's rule or recognizing the limit as a special one (limit of sinc function), we get:\[\lim_{{x  → 0}}  \frac{\sin(x)}{x} = \cos(0) = 1\]
      
      Explanation:
      The limit evaluates to 1 because sine approaches its linear approximation near 0, and the ratio of sine to x approaches the cosine function's value at 0.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake is to think that the limit is 0, which would be true if we were evaluating \(\lim_{{x  → 0}}  \frac{f(x)}{g(x)}\) where both f and g approach 0. However, in this case, the sine function approaches 0 while x also approaches 0, so the limit is not indeterminate.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 2 (:Q2):
      Evaluate the limit: \(\lim_{{x  →  \frac{\pi}{2}}}  \frac{1 -  \cos(x)}{x -  \frac{\pi}{2}}\)
      A) \(-1\)
      B) \(0\)
      C) \(1\)
      D) \(+\infty\)
      Correct Answer: A
      
      Solution:
      Using L'Hopital's rule, since the limit is in indeterminate form (difference of squares), we differentiate both numerator and denominator:\[\lim_{{x  →  \frac{\pi}{2}}}  \frac{1 -  \cos(x)}{x -  \frac{\pi}{2}} = \lim_{{x  →  \frac{\pi}{2}}}  \frac{\sin(x)}{1} = \sin\left(\frac{\pi}{2}\right) = 1\]
      
      Explanation:
      The limit evaluates to -1 because the numerator approaches 0 and the denominator approaches a constant, making the limit indeterminate. Applying L'Hopital's rule simplifies the problem to finding the sine of \(\frac{\pi}{2}\), which is 1.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake would be to evaluate the function at \(x =  \frac{\pi}{2}\) directly, without considering that the limit is in indeterminate form. This approach would lead to a value of 0/0, which is undefined.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 3 (:Q3):
      Find the limit: \(\lim_{{x  → 0}}  \frac{\tan(x) - x}{x^3}\)
      A) \(-\frac{1}{2}\)
      B) \(0\)
      C) \(\frac{1}{2}\)
      D) \(+\infty\)
      Correct Answer: A
      
      Solution:
      Using L'Hopital's rule twice, we differentiate numerator and denominator three times:\[\lim_{{x  → 0}}  \frac{\tan(x) - x}{x^3} = \lim_{{x  → 0}}  \frac{\sec^2(x) - 1}{3x^2} = \lim_{{x  → 0}}  \frac{2\sec^2(x)\tan(x)}{6x} = \lim_{{x  → 0}}  \frac{2\sec^4(x)}{6} = \frac{2}{6} = \frac{1}{3}\]
      
      Explanation:
      The limit evaluates to \(\frac{1}{3}\) because the numerator approaches 0 and the denominator approaches a constant, making the limit indeterminate. Applying L'Hopital's rule twice simplifies the problem to finding the limit of a rational function that can be evaluated directly.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake would be to stop after applying L'Hopital's rule once, which would lead to the limit being  \(\frac{2}{6} = \frac{1}{3}\) instead of the correct answer. It is important to continue differentiating until the limit becomes non-indeterminate.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 4 (:Q4):
      Evaluate the limit: \(\lim_{{x  → 0}}  \frac{\sin(x) - x}{x^3}\)
      A) \(-\frac{1}{6}\.
      B) \(0\)
      C) \(\frac{1}{6}\)
      D) \(+\infty\)
      Correct Answer: A
      
      Solution:
      Using L'Hopital's rule twice, we differentiate numerator and denominator three times:\[\lim_{{x  → 0}}  \frac{\sin(x) - x}{x^3} = \lim_{{x  → 0}}  \frac{\cos(x) - 1}{3x^2} = \lim_{{x  → 0}}  \frac{-\sin(x)}{6x} = \lim_{{x  → 0}}  \frac{-\cos(x)}{6} = -\frac{1}{6}\]
      
      Explanation:
      The limit evaluates to \(-\frac{1}{6}\) because the numerator approaches 0 and the denominator approaches a constant, making the limit indeterminate. Applying L'Hopital's rule twice simplifies the problem to finding the limit of a rational function that can be evaluated directly.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake would be to stop after applying L'Hopital's rule once, which would lead to an incorrect limit. It is important to continue differentiating until the limit becomes non-indeterminate.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 5 (:Q5):
      Find the limit: \(\lim_{{x  → 0}}  \frac{\sin(x) - x}{x^2}\)
      A) \(-1\)
      B) \(0\)
      C) \(1\)
      D) \(+\infty\)
      Correct Answer: A
      
      Solution:
      Using L'Hopital's rule, we differentiate numerator and denominator twice:\[\lim_{{x  → 0}}  \frac{\sin(x) - x}{x^2} = \lim_{{x  → 0}}  \frac{\cos(x) - 1}{2x} = \lim_{{x  → 0}}  \frac{-\sin(x)}{2} = 0\]
      
      Explanation:
      The limit evaluates to 0 because the numerator approaches 0 and the denominator approaches a constant, making the limit indeterminate. Applying L'Hopital's rule simplifies the problem to finding the limit of a rational function that can be evaluated directly.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake would be to think that the limit is -1 because the numerator approaches -x and the denominator approaches x^2, which could lead to an incorrect conclusion. It is important to use L'Hopital's rule correctly.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 6 (:Q6):
      Evaluate the limit: \(\lim_{{x  → 0}}  \frac{\tan(x) - x}{x^3}\)
      A) \(-\frac{1}{2}\.
      B) \(0\)
      C) \(\frac{1}{2}\)
      D) \(+\infty\)
      Correct Answer: A
      
      Solution:
      Using L'Hopital's rule twice, we differentiate numerator and denominator three times:\[\lim_{{x  → 0}}  \frac{\tan(x) - x}{x^3} = \lim_{{x  → 0}}  \frac{\sec^2(x) - 1}{3x^2} = \lim_{{x  → 0}}  \frac{2\sec^2(x)\tan(x)}{6x} = \lim_{{x  → 0}}  \frac{2\sec^4(x)}{6} = \frac{2}{6} = \frac{1}{3}\]
      
      Explanation:
      The limit evaluates to \(\frac{1}{3}\) because the numerator approaches 0 and the denominator approaches a constant, making the limit indeterminate. Applying L'Hopital's rule twice simplifies the problem to finding the limit of a rational function that can be evaluated directly.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake would be to stop after applying L'Hopital's rule once, which would lead to the limit being  \(\frac{2}{6} = \frac{1}{3}\) instead of the correct answer. It is important to continue differentiating until the limit becomes non-indeterminate.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 7 (:Q7):
      Find the limit: \(\lim_{{x  → 0}}  \frac{\sin(x)^2 - x^2}{x^4}\)
      A) \(-\frac{1}{3}\.
      B) \(0\)
      C) \(\frac{1}{3}\)
      D) \(+\infty\)
      Correct Answer: A
      
      Solution:
      Using L'Hopital's rule twice, we differentiate numerator and denominator four times:\[\lim_{{x  → 0}}  \frac{\sin(x)^2 - x^2}{x^4} = \lim_{{x  → 0}}  \frac{2\sin(x)\cos(x) - 2x}{4x^3} = \lim_{{x  → 0}}  \frac{2\cos^2(x) - 2}{12x^2} = \lim_{{x  → 0}}  \frac{-4\sin(x)\cos(x)}{24x} = \lim_{{x  → 0}}  \frac{-2\sin(2x)}{24} = -\frac{1}{3}\]
      
      Explanation:
      The limit evaluates to \(-\frac{1}{3}\) because the numerator approaches 0 and the denominator approaches a constant, making the limit indeterminate. Applying L'Hopital's rule twice simplifies the problem to finding the limit of a rational function that can be evaluated directly.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake would be to stop after applying L'Hopital's rule once or twice, which would lead to an incorrect limit. It is important to continue differentiating until the limit becomes non-indeterminate.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      1.5.1.10 Evaluate limits with exponential functions
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
==============================================================================
      LEARNING OBJECTIVE: 1.5.1.10 Evaluate limits with exponential functions
      ==============================================================================
      Context: 1.5 Calculus (Single Variable) > 1.5.1 Limits and Continuity
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      Difficulty profile: easy→hard mixed
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      Problem 1 (What is the limit of (2^x - 4) / x as x approaches 0?):
      (A) -4
      (B) 0
      (C) 2
      (D) ∞
      (E) Does not exist
      A) -4
      B) 0
      C) 2
      D) ∞ (infinity)
      Correct Answer: C
      
      Solution:
      The limit can be evaluated by applying L'Hôpital's rule, which states that if the limit of the ratio of the derivatives is finite and non-zero, then the original limit is also equal to that value. The derivative of 2^x is ln(2) 	imes 2^x, and the derivative of x is 1. Thus, we have the following:
      
      lim (x→0) [(2^x - 4) / x] = lim (x→0) [ln(2) 	imes 2^x / 1]
      = ln(2) 	imes 2^0
      = ln(2) 	imes 1
      = ln(2)
      
      Since ln(2) is a finite, non-zero value, the limit of (2^x - 4) / x as x approaches 0 is ln(2), which is approximately 0.69315.
      
      Explanation:
      The correct answer is C because L'Hôpital's rule simplifies the limit to a finite value.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake would be to assume that since 2^x approaches 1 and x approaches 0, the entire fraction approaches 0/0, which would lead to an indeterminate form. However, L'Hôpital's rule allows us to evaluate this limit by differentiating both the numerator and denominator.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 2 (What is the limit of (3^(2x) - 9) / x as x approaches 0?):
      (A) 6
      (B) 18
      (C) 4.5
      (D) ∞
      (E) Does not exist
      A) 6
      B) 18
      C) 4.5
      D) ∞ (infinity)
      Correct Answer: A
      
      Solution:
      Again, we can use L'Hôpital's rule to evaluate the limit. The derivative of 3^(2x) is ln(3) 	imes 2 	imes 3^(2x), and the derivative of x is 1. So we have:
      
      lim (x→0) [(3^(2x) - 9) / x] = lim (x→0) [ln(3) 	imes 2 	imes 3^(2x) / 1]
      = ln(3) 	imes 2 	imes 3^0
      = ln(3) 	imes 2 	imes 1
      = 2ln(3)
      
      Since ln(3) is approximately 1.0986, the limit of (3^(2x) - 9) / x as x approaches 0 is 2ln(3), which is approximately 2.1973.
      
      Explanation:
      The correct answer is A because L'Hôpital's rule simplifies the limit to a finite value.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake would be to try to simplify the expression by dividing both parts of the numerator by x, which would lead to an indeterminate form. However, this approach does not account for the change in the exponent when dividing 3^(2x) by x.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 3 (What is the limit of (e^x - 1) / x as x approaches 0?):
      (A) 1
      (B) 0.5
      (C) 2
      (D) ∞
      (E) Does not exist
      A) 1
      B) 0.5
      C) 2
      D) ∞ (infinity)
      Correct Answer: A
      
      Solution:
      This limit is a well-known limit that represents the derivative of e^x at x = 0, which is equal to the base e itself. By L'Hôpital's rule or by recognizing this special case, we know that:
      
      lim (x→0) [(e^x - 1) / x] = e^(0) 	imes 1
      = 1 	imes 1
      = 1
      
      Explanation:
      The correct answer is A because the limit represents the derivative of e^x evaluated at x = 0.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake would be to try to use a numerical approach or graphing method, which might give an approximate value close to 1 but not the exact value. The limit is exact and can be found using L'Hôpital's rule or recognizing the special case.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 4 (What is the limit of (5^x - 25) / x as x approaches 0?):
      (A) 4
      (B) 10
      (C) 2
      (D) ∞
      (E) Does not exist
      A) 4
      B) 10
      C) 2
      D) ∞ (infinity)
      Correct Answer: C
      
      Solution:
      We can use L'Hôpital's rule again. The derivative of 5^x is ln(5) 	imes 5^x, and the derivative of x is 1. Applying L'Hpital's rule gives us:
      
      lim (x→0) [(5^x - 25) / x] = lim (x→0) [ln(5) 	imes 5^x / 1]
      = ln(5) 	imes 5^0
      = ln(5) 	imes 1
      = ln(5)
      
      Since ln(5) is approximately 1.6094, the limit of (5^x - 25) / x as x approaches 0 is ln(5), which is approximately 1.6094.
      
      Explanation:
      The correct answer is C because L'Hôpital's rule simplifies the limit to a finite value.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake would be to try to simplify the expression by dividing both parts of the numerator by x, which would lead to an indeterminate form. However, this approach does not account for the change in the exponent when dividing 5^x by x.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 5 (What is the limit of (7^(3x) - 343) / x as x approaches 0?):
      (A) 63
      (B) 189
      (C) 21
      (D) ∞
      (E) Does not exist
      A) 63
      B) 189
      C) 21
      D) ∞ (infinity)
      Correct Answer: C
      
      Solution:
      Using L'Hôpital's rule, the derivative of 7^(3x) is ln(7) 	imes 3 	imes 7^(3x), and the derivative of x is 1. Applying L'Hpital's rule gives us:
      
      lim (x→0) [(7^(3x) - 343) / x] = lim (x→0) [ln(7) 	imes 3 	imes 7^(3x) / 1]
      = ln(7) 	imes 3 	imes 7^0
      = ln(7) 	imes 3 	imes 1
      = 3ln(7)
      
      Since ln(7) is approximately 1.9459, the limit of (7^(3x) - 343) / x as x approaches 0 is 3ln(7), which is approximately 5.8377.
      
      Explanation:
      The correct answer is C because L'Hôpital's rule simplifies the limit to a finite value.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake would be to try to simplify the expression by dividing both parts of the numerator by x, which would lead to an indeterminate form. However, this approach does not account for the change in the exponent when dividing 7^(3x) by x.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 6 (What is the limit of (e^(2x) - 1) / x as x approaches 0?):
      (A) 2
      (B) 1
      (C) 4
      (D) ∞
      (E) Does not exist
      A) 2
      B) 1
      C) 4
      D) ∞ (infinity)
      Correct Answer: B
      
      Solution:
      This limit is a special case that represents the derivative of e^(2x) at x = 0, which is equal to twice the base e. By L'Hôpital's rule or recognizing this special case, we know that:
      
      lim (x→0) [(e^(2x) - 1) / x] = 2 	imes e^(0) 	imes 1
      = 2 	imes 1 	imes 1
      = 2
      
      Explanation:
      The correct answer is B because the limit represents the derivative of e^(2x) evaluated at x = 0.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake would be to try to use a numerical approach or graphing method, which might give an approximate value close to 2 but not the exact value. The limit is exact and can be found using L'Hôpital's rule or recognizing the special case.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 7 (What is the limit of (9^(4x) - 6561) / x as x approaches 0?):
      (A) 27
      (B) 81
      (C) 36
      (D) ∞
      (E) Does not exist
      A) 27
      B) 81
      C) 36
      D) ∞ (infinity)
      Correct Answer: C
      
      Solution:
      Using L'Hôpital's rule, the derivative of 9^(4x) is ln(9) 	imes 4 	imes 9^(4x), and the derivative of x is 1. Applying L'Hpital's rule gives us:
      
      lim (x→0) [(9^(4x) - 6561) / x] = lim (x→0) [ln(9) 	imes 4 	imes 9^(4x) / 1]
      = ln(9) 	imes 4 	imes 9^0
      = ln(9) 	imes 4 	imes 1
      = 4ln(9)
      
      Since ln(9) is approximately 2.1973, the limit of (9^(4x) - 6561) / x as x approaches 0 is 4ln(9), which is approximately 8.7892.
      
      Explanation:
