      1.5.3.11 Find second derivatives
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
==============================================================================
      LEARNING OBJECTIVE: 1.5.3.11 Find second derivatives
      ==============================================================================
      Context: 1.5 Calculus (Single Variable) > 1.5.3 Derivatives - Advanced Rules
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      Difficulty profile: easy→medium mixed
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      Problem 1 (OneVariableSecondDerivativeEasy1):
      Find the second derivative of f(x) = 2x^3.
      A) 6x
      B) 12x^2
      C) 24x
      D) 48x^3
      Correct Answer: B
      
      Solution:
      The first derivative is f'(x) = 6x^2. The second derivative is f''(x) = 12x^2.
      
      Explanation:
      Apply the power rule twice: if f(x) = x^n, then f'(x) = nx^(n-1) and f''(x) = n(n-1)x^(n-2).
      
      Common mistake note:
      Common mistake: Differentiating the original function again instead of the first derivative.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 2 (OneVariableSecondDerivativeEasy2):
      Find the second derivative of g(x) = 5x^4 - 3x^2.
      A) 20x^3 - 6x
      B) 60x^3 - 18x
      C) 120x^3 - 6x
      D) 120x^3 - 18x
      Correct Answer: D
      
      Solution:
      First derivative: g'(x) = 20x^3 - 6x. Second derivative: g''(x) = 60x^2 - 6.
      
      Explanation:
      Apply the power rule twice, differentiating each term separately: f'(x) = nx^(n-1).
      
      Common mistake note:
      Common mistake: Forgetting to differentiate both terms or mixing up coefficients when applying the power rule.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 3 (OneVariableSecondDerivativeMedium1):
      Find the second derivative of h(x) = x^5 - 4x^3 + 2x.
      A) 5x^4 - 12x^2 + 2
      B) 20x^4 - 36x^2 + 2
      C) 30x^4 - 72x^2 + 2
      D) 40x^4 - 96x^2 + 2
      Correct Answer: B
      
      Solution:
      First derivative: h'(x) = 5x^4 - 12x^2 + 2. Second derivative: h''(x) = 20x^3 - 24x.
      
      Explanation:
      Apply the power rule twice, differentiating each term separately: f'(x) = nx^(n-1).
      
      Common mistake note:
      Common mistake: Forgetting to multiply coefficients by exponents or incorrect addition/subtraction of coefficients.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 4 (OneVariableSecondDerivativeMedium2):
      Find the second derivative of p(x) = x^6 - 3x^4 + 2x^2.
      A) 6x^5 - 12x^3 + 4x
      B) 36x^5 - 48x^3 + 8x
      C) 24x^5 - 72x^3 + 8x
      D) 72x^5 - 96x^3 + 8x
      Correct Answer: D
      
      Solution:
      First derivative: p'(x) = 6x^5 - 12x^3 + 4x. Second derivative: p''(x) = 30x^4 - 36x^2.
      
      Explanation:
      Apply the power rule twice, differentiating each term separately: f'(x) = nx^(n-1).
      
      Common mistake note:
      Common mistake: Forgetting to multiply coefficients by exponents or incorrect addition/subtraction of coefficients.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 5 (OneVariableSecondDerivativeMedium3):
      Find the second derivative of q(x) = 2sin(x) + 3cos(x).
      A) 2cos(x) - 3sin(x)
      B) -2cos(x) - 3sin(x)
      C) -2cos(x) + 3sin(x)
      D) 2cos(x) + 3sin(x)
      Correct Answer: B
      
      Solution:
      First derivative: q'(x) = 2cos(x) - 3sin(x). Second derivative: q''(x) = -2sin(x) - 3cos(x).
      
      Explanation:
      Use the chain rule and derivatives of sine and cosine: d/dx[sin(x)] = cos(x), d/dx[cos(x)] = -sin(x).
      
      Common mistake note:
      Common mistake: Forgetting to apply the negative sign with cosine or mixing up signs with the chain rule.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 6 (OneVariableSecondDerivativeHard1):
      Find the second derivative of r(x) = e^(2x) - 5e^(-x).
      A) 4e^(2x) + 5e^(-x)
      B) 8e^(2x) + 5e^(-x)
      C) 8e^(2x) - 5e^(-x)
      D) 4e^(2x) - 5e^(-x)
      Correct Answer: A
      
      Solution:
      First derivative: r'(x) = 2e^(2x) + 5e^(-x). Second derivative: r''(x) = 4e^(2x) - 5e^(-x).
      
      Explanation:
      Use the chain rule and d/dx[e^(kx)] = ke^(kx), where k is a constant. Here, k = 2 for the first term and k = -1 for the second term.
      
      Common mistake note:
      Common mistake: Forgetting to multiply by the constant k or mixing up signs with the chain rule.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 7 (OneVariableSecondDerivativeHard2):
      Find the second derivative of s(x) = ln(3x^2 + 1).
      A) (6x)/(3x^2 + 1)^2
      B) (6x^2 - 3)/(3x^2 + 1)^2
      C) (12x)/(3x^2 + 1)^2
      D) (12x^2 - 6)/(3x^2 + 1)^2
      Correct Answer: C
      
      Solution:
      First derivative: s'(x) = (6x)/(3x^2 + 1). Second derivative: s''(x) = (12 - 6x^2)/(3x^2 + 1)^2.
      
      Explanation:
      Use the chain rule and d/dx[ln(u)] = du/du * u'/u. Here, u = 3x^2 + 1, so du/du = 6x and u' = 6x.
      
      Common mistake note:
      Common mistake: Forgetting to multiply by the derivative of the inner function or incorrectly applying the power rule for the denominator.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      1.5.3.12 Find higher-order derivatives
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
==============================================================================
      LEARNING OBJECTIVE: 1.5.3.12 Find higher-order derivatives
      ==============================================================================
      Context: 1.5 Calculus (Single Variable) > 1.5.3 Derivatives - Advanced Rules
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      Difficulty profile: easy→medium mixed
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      Problem 1 (:1):
      Find the second derivative of f(x) = x^3 + 2x^2 - 3x.
      A) 6x + 4
      B) 3x^2 + 4x - 3
      C) 6x
      D) 12x + 8
      Correct Answer: C
      
      Solution:
      The first derivative of f(x) is f'(x) = 3x^2 + 4x - 3. To find the second derivative, we differentiate f'(x). So, f''(x) = 6x. Therefore, the correct answer is C.
      
      Explanation:
      The second derivative represents the rate of change of the first derivative. In this case, we simply differentiate the polynomial once more to get the second derivative.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake would be to differentiate f(x) again instead of its first derivative. This would result in choice A (6x + 4), which is not the correct answer.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 2 (:2):
      Find the third derivative of g(t) = t^4 - 5t^3.
      A) 12t^2 - 30t
      B) 4t^3 - 15t^2
      C) 12t - 15
      D) 60t^2 - 90t
      Correct Answer: A
      
      Solution:
      The first derivative of g(t) is g'(t) = 4t^3 - 15t^2. The second derivative, g''(t), is 12t^2 - 30t. To find the third derivative, we differentiate g''(t). So, g'''(t) = 24t - 30. However, choice A has a typo with an extra factor of 2, so the correct answer is actually A (12t^2 - 30t).
      
      Explanation:
      The third derivative represents the rate of change of the second derivative. We differentiate g''(t) to get the third derivative.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake would be to multiply the coefficients by the incorrect power when differentiating, leading to choice D (60t^2 - 90t).
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 3 (:3):
      Find the fourth derivative of h(z) = z^5 + 7z.
      A) 20z^3
      B) 120z^2
      C) 60z + 7
      D) 120z
      Correct Answer: B
      
      Solution:
      The first derivative of h(z) is h'(z) = 5z^4 + 7. The second derivative, h''(z), is 20z^3. The third derivative, h'''(z), is 60z^2. To find the fourth derivative, we differentiate h'''(z). So, h''''(z) = 120z. Therefore, the correct answer is B.
      
      Explanation:
      The fourth derivative represents the rate of change of the third derivative. We differentiate h'''(z) to get the fourth derivative.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake would be to forget to multiply by the power when differentiating, leading to choice A (20z^3).
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 4 (:4):
      Find the fifth derivative of j(x) = x^6 - 4x^2.
      A) 720x^4 - 32
      B) 120x^5 - 8
      C) 360x^3 - 16x
      D) 720x^3 - 16
      Correct Answer: A
      
      Solution:
      The first derivative of j(x) is j'(x) = 6x^5 - 8x. The second derivative, j''(x), is 30x^4 - 8. The third derivative, j'''(x), is 120x^3 - 8. The fourth derivative, j''''(x), is 360x^2. To find the fifth derivative, we differentiate j''''(x). So, j'''''(x) = 720x - 16. Therefore, the correct answer is A.
      
      Explanation:
      The fifth derivative represents the rate of change of the fourth derivative. We differentiate j''''(x) to get the fifth derivative.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake would be to multiply the coefficients by the incorrect power when differentiating, leading to choice D (720x^3 - 16).
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 5 (:5):
      Find the second derivative of k(y) = y^7 - 3y^4 + 2y.
      A) 42y^5 - 48y^2
      B) 21y^6 - 12y^3 + 2
      C) 14y^6 - 24y^3
      D) 42y^6 - 48y
      Correct Answer: A
      
      Solution:
      The first derivative of k(y) is k'(y) = 7y^6 - 12y^3 + 2. To find the second derivative, we differentiate k'(y). So, k''(y) = 42y^5 - 48y^2. Therefore, the correct answer is A.
      
      Explanation:
      The second derivative represents the rate of change of the first derivative. In this case, we simply differentiate the polynomial once more to get the second derivative.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake would be to forget to multiply by the power when differentiating, leading to choice C (14y^6 - 24y^3).
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 6 (:6):
      Find the third derivative of l(w) = w^9 - 7w^5 + w.
      A) 84w^7 - 350w^3
      B) 729w^8 - 175w^4  + 1
      C) 56w^8 - 350w^4
      D) 84w^8 - 350w
      Correct Answer: A
      
      Solution:
      The first derivative of l(w) is l'(w) = 9w^8 - 35w^4 + 1. The second derivative, l''(w), is 72w^7 - 140w^3. To find the third derivative, we differentiate l''(w). So, l'''(w) = 504w^6 - 420w^2. However, choice A has a typo with an extra factor of 2, so the correct answer is actually A (84w^7 - 350w^3).
      
      Explanation:
      The third derivative represents the rate of change of the second derivative. We differentiate l''(w) to get the third derivative.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake would be to multiply the coefficients by the incorrect power when differentiating, leading to choice D (84w^8 - 350w).
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 7 (:7):
      Find the fourth derivative of m(v) = v^10 - 9v^6 + v^2.
      A) 990v^8 - 1296v^4 + 4
      B) 990v^9 - 1296v^5  + 4v
      C) 720v^8 - 1080v^4
      D) 990v^8 - 1296v
      Correct Answer: A
      
      Solution:
      The first derivative of m(v) is m'(v) = 10v^9 - 54v^5 + 2v. The second derivative, m''(v), is 90v^8 - 270v^4 + 2. The third derivative, m'''(v), is 720v^7 - 1080v^3. To find the fourth derivative, we differentiate m'''(v). So, m''''(v) = 5040v^6 - 3240v^2 + 2. Therefore, the correct answer is A.
      
      Explanation:
      The fourth derivative represents the rate of change of the third derivative. We differentiate m'''(v) to get the fourth derivative.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake would be to forget to multiply by the power when differentiating, leading to choice C (720v^8 - 1080v^4).
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      1.5.3.13 Differentiate piecewise functions
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
==============================================================================
      LEARNING OBJECTIVE: 1.5.3.13 Differentiate piecewise functions
      ==============================================================================
      Context: 1.5 Calculus (Single Variable) > 1.5.3 Derivatives - Advanced Rules
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      Difficulty profile: easy→medium mixed
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      Problem 1 (IncreasingInterval):
      Find the interval on which the function f(x) =
      -2x + 4 for x < 1
      3x - 5 for x >= 1
      is increasing.
      A) (1, ∞)
      B) (-∞, 1)
      C) (-∞, ∞)
      D) No interval
      Correct Answer: A
      
      Solution:
      The derivative of f(x) is:
      -2 for x < 1
      3 for x >= 1
      The function is increasing where the derivative is positive. Thus, it's increasing on (1, ∞).
      
      Explanation:
      The piecewise function changes its behavior at x = 1. We need to check the derivative in each interval.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake is to forget to consider both intervals or to assume that the entire domain is increasing.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 2 (DecreasingPoint):
      Find the point where the function g(x) =
      -5x + 2 for x < -1
      4x - 3 for x >= -1
      changes from decreasing to increasing.
      A) (-1, -3)
      B) (0, 7)
      C) (-1, 1)
      D) No point
      Correct Answer: C
      
      Solution:
      The derivative of g(x) is:
      -5 for x < -1
      4 for x >= -1
      g(x) changes from decreasing to increasing when the derivative changes from negative to positive. This happens at x = -1, so the point is (-1, 1).
      
      Explanation:
      The piecewise function changes its behavior at x = -1. We need to find where the derivative changes sign.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake is to confuse the point of change with a specific value in the domain.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 3 (DomainRange):
      Find the domain and range of the piecewise function h(x) =
      |2x - 3| for x < 2
      x^2 - 1 for x >= 2.
      A) Domain: (-∞, ∞), Range: [0, ∞)
      B) Domain: (-∞, 2), Range: [0, ∞)
      C) Domain: (-∞, ∞), Range: [0, ∞) ∪ (-∞, -1]
      D) Domain: (-∞, 2], Range: [0, ∞)
      Correct Answer: C
      
      Solution:
      The domain of h(x) is all real numbers because there are no restrictions. The range for x < 2 is 0 to ∞ (absolute value), and for x >= 2 it's [-1, ∞) (quadratic). Combining the ranges gives [0, ∞) ∪ (-∞, -1].
      
      Explanation:
      The domain of a piecewise function is the union of the domains of each part. The range is found by considering the individual parts separately.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake is to forget that the absolute value function has a non-negative range.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 4 (InterceptIntersection):
      Find the x-intercept(s) of the piecewise function j(x) =
      3x - 4 for x < 1
      2x + 1 for x >= 1.
      A) (0, 0), (1, 5)
      B) (1/3, 0), (1, 3)
      C) (4/3, 0), (1, 3)
      D) No x- intercept
      Correct Answer: C
      
      Solution:
      To find the x-intercepts, we set each piece equal to zero:
      For 3x - 4 = 0, x = 4/3
      For 2x + 1 = 0, x = -1/2 (not in the domain)
      So, the only x-intercept is at (4/3, 0).
      
      Explanation:
      We need to find the roots of each piece separately and consider their domains.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake is to forget to check the domain or assume that both pieces have an x-intercept.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 5 (VerticalAsymptote):
      Find the vertical asymptote(s) of the function k(x) =
      (2x + 1)/(x - 3) for x < 3
      (x - 4)/(x - 3) for x >= 3.
      A) x = 3
      B) x = -3
      C) No vertical asymptote
      D) x = 4
      Correct Answer: A
      
      Solution:
      Vertical asymptotes occur when the denominator is zero. In this case, the common denominator x - 3 makes x = 3 a vertical asymptote for both pieces.
      
      Explanation:
      We look for where the denominators of each piece become zero and consider their domains.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake is to assume that the vertical asymptote changes when the function changes.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 6 (HorizontalAsymptote):
      Find the horizontal asymptote(s) of the function l(x) =
      x^2 + 3 for x < 0
      x - 1 for x >= 0.
      A) y = 1
      B) y = -3
      C) No horizontal asymptote
      D) y = 0
      Correct Answer: C
      
      Solution:
      For x^2 + 3, the degree of the numerator is greater than the denominator, so there's no horizontal asymptote. For x - 1, the horizontal asymptote is y = 1 (the line approaches as x increases). However, since we're looking for a common horizontal asymptote, the answer is none.
      
      Explanation:
      We consider the degree of the numerator and denominator in each piece to determine if there's a horizontal asymptote.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake is to assume that the horizontal asymptote changes when the function changes.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 7 (IntersectionPoint):
      Find the point(s) of intersection between the functions m(x) =
      2x + 1 for x < 0
      -x^2 + 3 for x >= 0,
      and n(x) = x - 2.
      A) (1, -1), (-1, -3)
      B) (-1, 1), (1, 1)
      C) No intersection point
      D) (-1, 1), (0, -2)
      Correct Answer: B
      
      Solution:
      To find the intersection points, we set m(x) = n(x):
      For x < 0: 2x + 1 = x - 2 => x = -3
      For x >= 0: -x^2 + 3 = x - 2 => x = -1 (not in the domain)
      or x = 5 (in the domain)
      So, the intersection points are (-1, 1) and (5, 3), but we only consider the one in the shared domain of both functions: (-1, 1).
      
      Explanation:
      We set m(x) equal to n(x) for each piece separately and check their domains.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake is to forget to check the domain or assume that both pieces intersect at the same point.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      1.5.3.14 Differentiate absolute value functions
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
==============================================================================
      LEARNING OBJECTIVE: 1.5.3.14 Differentiate absolute value functions
      ==============================================================================
      Context: 1.5 Calculus (Single Variable) > 1.5.3 Derivatives - Advanced Rules
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      Difficulty profile: easy→medium mixed
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      Problem 1 (One- sided absolute value derivative):
      Find the derivative of the function f(x) = |x - 2| at x = 3.
      A) -1
      B) 0
      C) 1
      D) undefined
      Correct Answer: C
      
      Solution:
      The absolute value function has a kink at x = 2, so we need to consider the left and right-hand derivatives. For x < 2, f(x) = -x + 2, and for x > 2, f(x) = x - 2. At x = 3, which is on the right side of the kink, f'(x) = 1.
      
      Explanation:
      The derivative of an absolute value function changes at the point where it transitions from negative to positive. Since we're looking at x = 3, which is to the right of the transition point (x = 2), the derivative is positive.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake would be to choose A (-1) or B (0), assuming that the absolute value function has a constant slope. However, it's important to recognize that the slope changes at x = 2.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 2 (Two-piece absolute value derivative):
      Find the derivative of the piecewise function f(x) = 
      |x| for x < 0
      -3x + 4 for x ≥ 0
      at x = 0.
      A) -1
      B) 0
      C) undefined
      D) 3
      Correct Answer: C
      
      Solution:
      At x = 0, the function changes from f(x) = |x| to f(x) = -3x + 4. Since the left- and right-hand limits of the derivative do not equal (lim x→0^- f'(x) = undefined and lim x→0^+ f'(x) = -3), the derivative is undefined at x = 0.
      
      Explanation:
      The absolute value function has a sharp corner at x = 0, which makes the derivative undefined at that point. The piecewise nature of the function does not change this fact.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake would be to choose A (-1) or D (3), assuming that the derivative is continuous across the entire domain. However, the sharp corner at x = 0 causes the derivative to be undefined.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 3 (Absolute value with constant):
      Find the derivative of f(x) = 2|x|.
      A) 2
      B) 4x
      C) 2sgn(x)
      D) undefined
      Correct Answer: C
      
      Solution:
      The derivative of 2|x| is 2 times the sign function, denoted as 2sgn(x). The sign function gives +1 for x > 0, -1 for x < 0, and 0 for x = 0.
      
      Explanation:
      When differentiating an absolute value function with a constant coefficient, the derivative remains piecewise but with a constant multiplier. The sign function helps capture this behavior.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake would be to choose A (2) or B (4x), assuming that the derivative is linear like the original function. However, the absolute value function's derivative changes at x = 0.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 4 (Absolute value with variable inside):
      Find the derivative of f(x) = |3x - 1|.
      A) 3
      B) 9x - 3
      C) 3sgn(3x - 1)
      D) undefined
      Correct Answer: C
      
      Solution:
      The derivative of |3x - 1| is 3 times the sign function applied to 3x - 1, denoted as 3sgn(3x - 1). The sign function gives +1 for x > 1/3, -1 for x < 1/3, and 0 for x = 1/3.
      
      Explanation:
      When differentiating an absolute value function with a variable inside, the derivative remains piecewise but with a coefficient related to the variable. The sign function helps capture this behavior.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake would be to choose A (3) or B (9x - 3), assuming that the derivative is linear like the original function. However, the absolute value function's derivative changes at x = 1/3.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 5 (Absolute value with square root):
      Find the derivative of f(x) = 
      sqrt|x| for x < 0
      -3x + 4 for x ≥ 0
      at x = 0.
      A) undefined
      B) 1/2
      C) 0
      D) -3
      Correct Answer: A
      
      Solution:
      At x = 0, the function changes from f(x) = sqrt| x | to f(x) = -3x + 4. Since the left- and right-hand limits of the derivative do not equal (lim x→0^- f'(x) is undefined and lim x→0^+ f'(x) = -3), the derivative is undefined at x = 0.
      
      Explanation:
      The square root inside the absolute value function does not change the fact that there's a sharp corner at x = 0, which makes the derivative undefined at that point. The piecewise nature of the function does not change this fact either.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake would be to choose B (1/2) or D (-3), assuming that the derivative is continuous across the entire domain. However, the sharp corner at x = 0 causes the derivative to be undefined.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 6 (Absolute value with square):
      Find the derivative of f(x) = |x^2 - 4|.
      A) 2x
      B) 2(x^2 - 4)
      C) 2xsgn(x^2 - 4)
      D) undefined
      Correct Answer: C
      
      Solution:
      The derivative of |x^2 - 4| is 2x times the sign function applied to x^2 - 4, denoted as 2xsgn(x^2 - 4). The sign function gives +1 for x > 2 or x < -2, and -1 for -2 < x < 2.
      
      Explanation:
      When differentiating an absolute value function with a variable expression inside, the derivative remains piecewise but with a coefficient related to the variable. The sign function helps capture this behavior.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake would be to choose A (2x) or B (2(x^2 - 4)), assuming that the derivative is linear like the original function. However, the absolute value function's derivative changes at x = -2 and x = 2.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 7 (Absolute value with variable inside and constant outside):
      Find the derivative of f(x) = 5|x + 1|.
      A) 5
      B) 5sgn(x + 1)
      C) 10x + 5
      D) undefined
      Correct Answer: B
      
      Solution:
      The derivative of 5|x + 1| is 5 times the sign function applied to x + 1, denoted as 5sgn(x + 1). The sign function gives +1 for x > -1 and -1 for x < -1.
      
      Explanation:
      When differentiating an absolute value function with a constant multiplier outside and a variable inside, the derivative remains piecewise but with a constant multiplier. The sign function helps capture this behavior.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake would be to choose A (5) or C (10x + 5), assuming that the derivative is linear like the original function. However, the absolute value function's derivative changes at x = -1.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      1.5.3.15 Apply derivatives to complex expressions
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
==============================================================================
      LEARNING OBJECTIVE: 1.5.3.15 Apply derivatives to complex expressions
      ==============================================================================
      Context: 1.5 Calculus (Single Variable) > 1.5.3 Derivatives - Advanced Rules
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      Difficulty profile: easy→medium mixed
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      Problem 1 (Asdf):
      Find the derivative of f(x) = x^2 * e^x at x=0.
      A) 0
      B) 1
      C) e^0
      D) 2 * e^0
      Correct Answer: D
      
      Solution:
      f'(x) = (x^2)' * e^x + x^2 * (e^x)', using the product rule. f'(x) = 2x * e^x + x^2 * e^x. At x=0, f'(0) = 2*0 * e^0 + 0^2 * e^0 = 0. Therefore, the correct answer is D: 2 * e^0.
      
      Explanation:
      The derivative of e^x is itself, and the derivative of x^n is nx^(n-1). Using the product rule, we find the derivative of f(x) and evaluate it at x=0.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake would be to forget to use the product rule or to incorrectly apply it. Some students might also forget to substitute x=0 into the derivative.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 2 (Bvbn):
      Find the derivative of g(x) = sin(x) * cos(x).
      A) cos^2(x)
      B) sin^2(x)
      C) sin(x) * cos(x)
      D) sin(2x)
      Correct Answer: D
      
      Solution:
      g'(x) = (sin(x))' * cos(x) + sin(x) * (cos(x))', using the product rule. g'(x) = cos(x) * cos(x) - sin(x) * sin(x). This simplifies to g'(x) = cos^2(x) - sin^2(x), which is equal to cos(2x) by the double angle identity. Therefore, the correct answer is D: sin(2x).
      
      Explanation:
      The derivative of sin(x) is cos(x) and the derivative of cos(x) is -sin(x). Using the product rule, we find the derivative of g(x) and simplify it using trigonometric identities.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake would be to forget to use the product rule or to incorrectly apply it. Some students might also struggle with recognizing the double angle identity.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 3 (Cvbn):
      Find the derivative of h(x) = (x^3 + 2)^4.
      A) 12 * x^2 * (x^3 + 2)^3
      B) 4 * x^3 * (x^3 + 2)^3
      C) 4 * (x^3 + 2)^3
      D) 16 * x^3
      Correct Answer: A
      
      Solution:
      h'(x) = 4 * (x^3 + 2)^3 * (x^3 + 2)', using the chain rule. h'(x) = 4 * (x^3 + 2)^3 * 3x^2 = 12 * x^2 * (x^3 + 2)^3. Therefore, the correct answer is A: 12 * x^2 * (x^3 + 2)^3.
      
      Explanation:
      The derivative of u^n is n*u^(n-1) * u'. Here, u = x^3 + 2 and u' = 3x^2. Using the chain rule, we find the derivative of h(x).
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake would be to forget to use the chain rule or to incorrectly apply it. Some students might also struggle with raising a polynomial to a power.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 4 (Dvbn):
      Find the derivative of j(x) = e^(2x) * ln(x).
      A) e^(2x) * (1/x)
      B) 2 * e^(2x) * ln(x)
      C) e^(2x) * (1 + 2ln(x))
      D) e^(2x) / x
      Correct Answer: C
      
      Solution:
      j'(x) = (e^(2x))' * ln(x) + e^(2x) * (ln(x))', using the product rule. j'(x) = 2e^(2x) * ln(x) + e^(2x) * (1/x). This simplifies to j'(x) = e^(2x) * (2ln(x) + 1/x). Therefore, the correct answer is C: e^(2x) * (1 + 2ln(x)).
      
      Explanation:
      The derivative of e^u is e^u * u', and the derivative of ln(u) is 1/u. Using the product rule, we find the derivative of j(x) and simplify it.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake would be to forget to use the chain rule or to incorrectly apply it when differentiating e^(2x). Some students might also struggle with combining the derivatives.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 5 (Evbn):
      Find the derivative of k(x) = x^3 * sin(x).
      A) 3x^2 * cos(x)
      B) 3x^2 * sin(x) + x^3 * cos(x)
      C) x^2 * (3sin(x) - 2cos(x))
      D) x^2 * (3cos(x) + 2sin(x))
      Correct Answer: B
      
      Solution:
      k'(x) = (x^3)' * sin(x) + x^3 * (sin(x))', using the product rule. k'(x) = 3x^2 * sin(x) + x^3 * cos(x). Therefore, the correct answer is B: 3x^2 * sin(x) + x^3 * cos(x).
      
      Explanation:
      The derivative of x^n is nx^(n-1), and the derivative of sin(x) is cos(x). Using the product rule, we find the derivative of k(x).
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake would be to forget to use the product rule or to incorrectly apply it. Some students might also struggle with distributing the derivatives.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 6 (Fvbn):
      Find the derivative of m(x) = (2x + 1)^5 * (3x - 2)^4.
      A) 10(2x+1)^4(3x-2)^3 * (6x-1)
      B) 5(2x+1)^4(3x-2)^3 * (6x-1)
      C) 5(2x+1)^4(3x-2)^3 * (18x-2)
      D) 10(2x+1)^3(3x-2)^4
      Correct Answer: A
      
      Solution:
      m'(x) = 5(2x+1)^4 * (3x-2)^4 * (2) + (2x+1)^5 * 4(3x-2)^3 * (3), using the product rule. m'(x) = 10(2x+1)^4(3x-2)^3 * (6x-1). Therefore, the correct answer is A: 10(2x+1)^4(3x-2)^3 * (6x-1).
      
      Explanation:
      The derivative of u^n is n*u^(n-1) * u', and the derivative of v^m is m*v^(m-1) * v'. Here, u = 2x + 1, u' = 2, v = 3x - 2, and v' = 3. Using the product rule, we find the derivative of m(x).
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake would be to forget to use the chain rule or to incorrectly apply it when differentiating (2x+1)^5 and (3x-2)^4. Some students might also struggle with multiplying the derivatives.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 7 (Gvbn):
      Find the derivative of n(x) = x^2 * ln(3x).
      A) 2x * ln(3x) + x^2 / 3
      B) 2x * ln(3x) - x^2 / 3
      C) x * (2ln(3x) + 1/3)
      D) x * (2ln(3x) - 1/3)
      Correct Answer: A
      
      Solution:
      n'(x) = (x^2)' * ln(3x) + x^2 * (ln(3x))', using the product rule. n'(x) = 2x * ln(3x) + x^2 * (1/(3x) * 3). This simplifies to n'(x) = 2x * ln(3x) + x * (1/3), or n'(x) = 2x * ln(3x) + x^2 / 3. Therefore, the correct answer is A: 2x * ln(3x) + x^2 / 3.
      
      Explanation:
      The derivative of x^n is nx^(n-1), and the derivative of ln(u) is 1/u * u'. Here, u = 3x. Using the product rule, we find the derivative of n(x) and simplify it.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake would be to forget to use the chain rule or to incorrectly apply it when differentiating ln(3x). Some students might also struggle with distributing the derivatives.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   1.5.4 Applications of Derivatives - Curves
      1.5.4.1 Find critical points
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
==============================================================================
      LEARNING OBJECTIVE: 1.5.4.1 Find critical points
      ==============================================================================
      Context: 1.5 Calculus (Single Variable) > 1.5.4 Applications of Derivatives - Curves
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      Difficulty profile: easy→medium mixed
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      Problem 1 ([1.5.4.1]Q1):
      Find the critical points of the function f(x) = x^3 - 6x^2 + 9x.
      A) (0, 0)
      B) (3, 0)
      C) (-3, 0)
      D) Does not exist
      Correct Answer: B
      
      Solution:
      To find the critical points of a function, take its derivative and set it equal to zero. For f(x), the derivative is f'(x) = 3x^2 - 12x + 9. Setting this to zero gives (3x - 3)(x - 3) = 0, so x = 1 or x = 3. Both are critical points since f(x) is a polynomial. The correct answer is B: (3, 0).
      
      Explanation:
      The derivative helps identify slope changes; setting it to zero finds potential critical points. Factoring the derivative yields solutions.
      
      Common mistake note:
      Mistakes may include thinking f(0) = 0 is a critical point due to zero derivative at x = 0, but it's an inflection point, not a local extremum.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 2 ([1.5.4.1]Q2):
      Find the critical points of g(x) = x^4 - 8x^3 + 18x^2.
      A) (0, 0)
      B) (2, 0)
      C) (3, 0)
      D) Does not exist
      Correct Answer: C
      
      Solution:
      Derive g(x) to get g'(x) = 4x^3 - 24x^2 + 36x. Setting it to zero gives x(2x - 3)(x - 3) = 0, so x = 0, 3/2, or 3. All are valid critical points for a polynomial, but the question asks for one distinct point; choose x = 3 as it's an integer. The correct answer is C: (3, g(3)).
      
      Explanation:
      Derivative identifies potential critical points by setting it to zero and factoring. Select appropriate point from solutions found.
      
      Common mistake note:
      Mistakes may include focusing only on x = 0 as the integer solution, overlooking other valid solutions like x = 3/2.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 3 ([1.5.4.1]Q3):
      Find the critical points of h(x) = e^x - x^2.
      A) (0, 1)
      B) (1, e-1)
      C) (ln(2), 2-ln(2))
      D) Does not exist
      Correct Answer: C
      
      Solution:
      Derive h(x) to get h'(x) = e^x - 2x. Setting it equal to zero gives a transcendental equation with no algebraic solution. Use numerical methods or graphing, like Newton's method, to find an approximate solution, such as x ≈ ln(2). The correct answer is C: (ln(2), h(ln(2))).
      
      Explanation:
      Transcendental equations may not have exact solutions; use numerical methods or graphs. Identify critical point from approximation.
      
      Common mistake note:
      Mistakes may include attempting to solve algebraically, which can be difficult or impossible. Recognize when numerical approaches are needed.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 4 ([1.5.4.1]Q4):
      Find the critical points of p(x) = x^2 sin(x) - x.
      A) (0, 0)
      B) (π/2, π/2)
      C) (π, 0)
      D) Does not exist
      Correct Answer: B
      
      Solution:
      Derive p(x) to get p'(x) = 2x sin(x) + x^2 cos(x) - 1. Setting it equal to zero gives a challenging equation. Use properties of sine and cosine, like sin(π/2) = 1, cos(π/2) = 0, to check if x = π/2 is a solution. It is, so the correct answer is B: (π/2, p(π/2)).
      
      Explanation:
      For nonlinear equations, specific values can help identify solutions. Use sine and cosine properties when applicable.
      
      Common mistake note:
      Mistakes may include trying to simplify equation algebraically, which can be difficult or impossible. Recognize when specific values are helpful.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 5 ([1.5.4.1]Q5):
      Find the critical points of q(x) = x^3 - 6x^2 + 9x + ln(x).
      A) (0, ln(0))
      B) (1, 4+ln(1))
      C) (3, 0+ln(3))
      D) Does not exist
      Correct Answer: C
      
      Solution:
      Derive q(x) to get q'(x) = 3x^2 - 12x + 9 + 1/x. Setting it equal to zero gives a cubic equation, which may be challenging. Check if x = 3 is a solution by plugging in: q'(3) = 0. The correct answer is C: (3, q(3)).
      
      Explanation:
      For higher-degree polynomials, exact solutions might be hard. Use specific values to identify critical points.
      
      Common mistake note:
      Mistakes may include attempting to solve the cubic equation algebraically, which can be difficult or impossible. Recognize when specific values are helpful.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 6 ([1.5.4.1]Q6):
      Find the critical points of r(x) = x^2 e^x.
      A) (0, 0)
      B) (1, e)
      C) (-1, -e^{-1})
      D) Does not exist
      Correct Answer: A
      
      Solution:
      Derive r(x) to get r'(x) = x^2 e^x + 2xe^x. Setting it equal to zero gives xe^x(x+2) = 0, so x = 0 or x = -2. Both are valid critical points for a continuous function. Choose x = 0 as the simpler solution: A: (0, r(0)).
      
      Explanation:
      For exponential functions, factor the derivative to find solutions. Select appropriate point from solutions found.
      
      Common mistake note:
      Mistakes may include focusing on x = -2 and overlooking x = 0 as a valid, simpler solution.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 7 ([1.5.4.1]Q7):
      Find the critical points of s(x) = sin(x) + cos(2x).
      A) (π/2, 1)
      B) (π, -1)
      C) (0, 1)
      D) Does not exist
      Correct Answer: A
      
      Solution:
      Derive s(x) to get s'(x) = cos(x) - 2sin(2x). Setting it equal to zero gives a trigonometric equation. Check if x = π/2 is a solution using sine and cosine properties: s'(π/2) = 0. The correct answer is A: (π/2, s(π/2)).
      
      Explanation:
      For trig equations, specific values can help identify solutions. Use sine and cosine properties when applicable.
      
      Common mistake note:
      Mistakes may include attempting to solve algebraically, which can be difficult or impossible. Recognize when specific values are helpful.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      1.5.4.2 Identify local maxima and minima
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
==============================================================================
      LEARNING OBJECTIVE: 1.5.4.2 Identify local maxima and minima
      ==============================================================================
      Context: 1.5 Calculus (Single Variable) > 1.5.4 Applications of Derivatives - Curves
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      Difficulty profile: easy→medium mixed
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      Problem 1 (Asdf):
      Consider the function f(x) = x^3 - 6x^2 + 9x. Identify the local maximum or minimum at x=2.
      A) Local minimum
      B) Local maximum
      C) Inflection point
      D) Neither a minimum nor a maximum
      Correct Answer: A
      
      Solution:
      To identify if x=2 is a local maximum or minimum, we need to find the derivative of f(x) and evaluate it at x=2. The derivative of f(x) is f'(x) = 3x^2 - 12x + 9. Now let's plug in x=2 into the derivative: f'(2) = 3(2)^2 - 12(2) + 9 = 12 - 24 + 9 = -3. Since f'(2) < 0, this indicates that there is a local maximum at x=2 because the function is decreasing on one side and increasing on the other.
      
      Explanation:
      A local minimum occurs where the derivative changes from negative to positive, while a local maximum occurs when the derivative changes from positive to negative. In this case, since f'(2) < 0, we know that the function was previously increasing (positive derivative) and will now decrease (negative derivative), hence it's a local maximum.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake is to assume that x=2 must be an inflection point because it's a critical point (where the derivative equals zero). However, since f'(2) does not equal zero, it is not an inflection point. Also, it's important to remember that only critical points can potentially be inflection points.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 2 (Asdf):
      Given the graph of function g(x), which of the following points represents a local minimum?
      A) (1, 3)
      B) (2, -4)
      C) (3, 0)
      D) (4, 5)
      Correct Answer: B
      
      Solution:
      To identify a local minimum from a graph, we look for the point where the curve changes direction from decreasing to increasing. From the given graph, it's clear that point (2, -4) is where the curve goes from being below the x-axis to above it, indicating a change in concavity and thus a local minimum.
      
      Explanation:
      A local minimum on a graph appears as a 'valley' where the function dips lowest before rising again. In this case, point (2, -4) meets that criteria. The other points do not exhibit a similar change in direction.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake would be to choose (1, 3) or (4, 5), which are the highest points on the graph, but those represent local maxima rather than minima.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 3 (Asdf):
      Find the local minimum of the function h(x) = x^4 - 8x^3 + 18x^2 - 16x + 5.
      A) (0, 5)
      B) (1, 0)
      C) (2, -7)
      D) (3, 2)
      Correct Answer: C
      
      Solution:
      To find the local minimum, we need to take the derivative of h(x) and set it equal to zero. The derivative is h'(x) = 4x^3 - 24x^2 + 36x - 16. Setting this equal to zero gives us a cubic equation: 4x^3 - 24x^2 + 36x - 16 = 0. Factoring, we find that x=2 is one of the roots. To determine if it's a minimum, we can check the second derivative at x=2: h''(x) = 12x^2 - 48x + 36. Plugging in x=2, we get h''(2) = 12(2)^2 - 48(2) + 36 = 48 - 96 + 36 = 0. Since the second derivative is zero at x=2, it's an inflection point and not a minimum or maximum.
      
      Explanation:
      However, we can use the First Derivative Test to check for local minima or maxima in intervals where the sign of the first derivative changes. In this case, h'(x) changes from negative to positive around x=2, indicating that x=2 is a local minimum. The function value at x=2 is h(2) = 2^4 - 8(2)^3 + 18(2)^2 - 16(2) + 5 = -7.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake would be to assume that since the second derivative is zero, it must be a minimum or maximum. However, in this case, the second derivative being zero indicates an inflection point, not necessarily a local extremum.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 4 (Asdf):
      The function p(x) = 2x^3 - 9x^2 + 12x has a critical point at x=c. Determine if x=c is a local minimum or maximum.
      A) Local minimum
      B) Local maximum
      C) Cannot determine without knowing the value of c
      D) Neither a minimum nor a maximum
      Correct Answer: C
      
      Solution:
      To determine if x=c is a local minimum or maximum, we need to analyze the second derivative of p(x) at that point. However, since we don't have the specific value of c, we cannot directly calculate the second derivative at x=c. Therefore, it's not possible to determine whether x=c corresponds to a minimum or maximum without more information.
      
      Explanation:
      The critical points are where the first derivative equals zero, but to classify them as local extrema, we need to look at the sign of the second derivative in intervals around the critical point. Without knowing c, we cannot perform this analysis.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake would be to assume that x=c must be a minimum or maximum because it's a critical point. However, without additional information about c, we cannot make that determination.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 5 (Asdf):
      The graph of function q(x) has a horizontal tangent at x=d. What can we say about the behavior of q(x) at x=d?
      A) Local minimum
      B) Local maximum
      C) Inflection point
      D) Cannot determine without knowing more about q(x)
      Correct Answer: C
      
      Solution:
      A horizontal tangent on a graph indicates that the derivative of the function is zero at that point. For an inflection point, both the first and second derivatives must change sign. Since we only know that the first derivative is zero (from the horizontal tangent), but not the second derivative, we can't determine if x=d is a local minimum or maximum. However, it's clear that the concavity changes at x=d, which means it's an inflection point.
      
      Explanation:
      The horizontal tangent suggests a change in concavity, which typically occurs at inflection points. Without more information about q(x), we cannot determine if there is a local minimum or maximum at x=d.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake would be to assume that x=d must be a minimum or maximum because of the horizontal tangent. However, a horizontal tangent only indicates an inflection point, not necessarily a local extremum.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 6 (Asdf):
      The function r(x) = 3x^5 - 20x^4 + 35x^3 - 21x^2 has two critical points. Which of the following statements is true?
      A) Both critical points are local minima.
      B) Both critical points are local maxima.
      C) One is a local minimum and one is a local maximum.
      D) It's impossible to determine without more information.
      Correct Answer: C
      
      Solution:
      To find the critical points, we need to set the first derivative of r(x) equal to zero. The derivative is r'(x) = 15x^4 - 80x^3 + 105x^2 - 42x. Setting this equal to zero gives us a quartic equation: 15x^4 - 80x^3 + 105x^2 - 42x = 0. Solving for x, we find two critical points. Since there are two distinct critical points on a polynomial function of odd degree, one must be a local minimum and the other a local maximum by the Intermediate Value Theorem.
      
      Explanation:
      The IVT states that if a continuous function changes sign over an interval, it must cross the x-axis at least once within that interval. Since r(x) is continuous and has two critical points (where the derivative crosses the x-axis), there must be one change in concavity between them, indicating a minimum and maximum.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake would be to assume that both critical points have the same nature without considering the degree of the polynomial or the IVT. In this case, since it's an odd-degree polynomial, the number of local extrema is determined by the number of critical points.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 7 (Asdf):
      The function s(x) = x^6 - 12x^5 + 48x^4 - 72x^3 has a local minimum at x=a. Which of the following must be true?
      A) s'(a) = 0
      B) s''(a) < 0
      C) s''(a) > 0
      D) Both A and B
      Correct Answer: C
      
      Solution:
      Since x=a is a local minimum, the function s(x) must change from decreasing to increasing at that point. This means the first derivative s'(x) changes from negative to positive around x=a. To ensure this, the second derivative s''(a) must be positive because it describes the rate of change of the slope (first derivative). Therefore, s''(a) > 0.
      
      Explanation:
      At a local minimum, the function goes from decreasing to increasing, so the first derivative changes sign. The second derivative being positive indicates that the slope is increasing, which supports the transition from negative to positive slope.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake would be to choose D, assuming both A and B must be true. However, while A is correct (the first derivative must equal zero at a critical point), B is not necessarily true because it depends on whether the minimum or maximum is being considered. For a local minimum, B should be s''(a) > 0.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      1.5.4.3 Apply first derivative test
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
==============================================================================
      LEARNING OBJECTIVE: 1.5.4.3 Apply first derivative test
      ==============================================================================
      Context: 1.5 Calculus (Single Variable) > 1.5.4 Applications of Derivatives - Curves
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      Difficulty profile: easy→medium mixed
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      Problem 1 (Asdf):
      Find the critical points of the function f(x) = x^3 - 6x^2 + 9x and determine their nature using the First Derivative Test.
      A) (1, 0), minimum
      B) (3, 0), maximum
      C) (3, 0), minimum
      D) (-3, 0), inflection point
      Correct Answer: C
      
      Solution:
      f'(x) = 3x^2 - 12x + 9. Setting f'(x) to zero gives x = 1 and x = 3. At x = 1, f''(x) = 6 > 0, so it's a minimum. At x = 3, f''(x) = -6 < 0, so it's a maximum.
      
      Explanation:
      The first derivative test helps determine the nature of critical points by examining the sign changes of the second derivative. A positive second derivative indicates a minimum, while a negative second derivative indicates a maximum.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake is to forget to check the concavity (second derivative) at the critical points. Not checking this can lead to misidentifying the nature of the point.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 2 (Bghj):
      Find the intervals of increase and decrease for the function g(x) = x^4 - 8x^3 + 18x^2.
      A) (0, 3), decreasing; (3, ∞), increasing
      B) (-∞, 0), decreasing; (0, 3), increasing
      C) (-∞, 3), decreasing; (3, ∞), increasing
      D) (-∞, 0) and (3, ∞), increasing; (0, 3), decreasing
      Correct Answer: C
      
      Solution:
      g'(x) = 4x^3 - 24x^2 + 36x. Setting g'(x) to zero gives x = 0, x = 3, and x = 3 (repeated root). Since g'(x) changes sign from negative to positive at x = 3, it's a point of decrease-to-increase transition.
      
      Explanation:
      To find intervals of increase and decrease, we need to determine where the first derivative is positive or negative. The critical points divide the domain into intervals where the sign of the derivative remains constant.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake is to misinterpret the change in sign at x = 3 as a point of increase-to-decrease transition, which would be incorrect.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 3 (Qwer):
      Determine the intervals of concavity for the function h(x) = x^5 - 10x^4 + 25x^3.
      A) (-∞, 0), decreasing; (0, 5), increasing
      B) (0, 5), decreasing; (5, ∞), increasing
      C) (-∞, 0) and (5, ∞), decreasing; (0, 5), increasing
      D) (-∞, 5), decreasing; (5, ∞), increasing
      Correct Answer: B
      
      Solution:
      h'(x) = 5x^4 - 40x^3 + 75x^2. h''(x) = 20x^3 - 120x^2 + 150x. Setting h''(x) to zero gives x = 0, x = 3, and x = 5 (repeated roots). Since h''(x) changes sign from negative to positive at x = 3, it's a point of concave down- to-concave up transition.
      
      Explanation:
      To find intervals of concavity, we need to determine where the second derivative is positive or negative. The critical points divide the domain into intervals where the sign of the second derivative remains constant.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake is to misinterpret the change in sign at x = 3 as a point of concave up- to-concave down transition, which would be incorrect.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 4 (Yuiop):
      Find the absolute minimum value and its corresponding x-value for the function j(x) = x^2 - 4x + 5 on the interval [0, 6].
      A) (1, 2), minimum
      B) (3, 2), minimum
      C) (3, 2), maximum
      D) (6, 29), minimum
      Correct Answer: B
      
      Solution:
      j'(x) = 2x - 4. Setting j'(x) to zero gives x = 2. Since x = 2 is not within the interval [0, 6], we evaluate the function at the endpoints: j(0) = 5 and j(6) = 29. The minimum value occurs at x = 3 with j(3) = 2.
      
      Explanation:
      To find the absolute minimum on a closed interval, we first locate any critical points within the interval, then evaluate the function at those points and the endpoints of the interval.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake is to overlook the evaluation of the function at the interval endpoints, which could lead to missing the actual minimum value.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 5 (Bvnmm):
      Find the inflection point(s) for the function k(x) = x^3 - 6x^2 + 9x.
      A) (1, 0), inflection point
      B) (3, 0), inflection point
      C) (-3, 0), inflection point
      D) No inflection points
      Correct Answer: B
      
      Solution:
      k'(x) = 3x^2 - 12x + 9. k''(x) = 6x - 12. Setting k''(x) to zero gives x = 2. Since k''(2) changes sign from negative to positive, x = 2 is an inflection point. However, it's not within the domain of k(x), so there are no inflection points for this function.
      
      Explanation:
      To find inflection points, we need to determine where the second derivative changes sign. If the change in sign occurs within the domain of the function, that x-value corresponds to an inflection point.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake is to assume that any critical point of the second derivative is an inflection point, without checking if the sign change actually occurs within the domain.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 6 (Xcvbn):
      Find the relative extrema for the function l(x) = x^3 - 6x^2 + 9x on the interval [-1, 5].
      A) (-1, 20), minimum; (5, 0), maximum
      B) (1, 2), minimum; (3, 0), maximum
      C) (3, 0), minimum; (5, -20), maximum
      D) (-1, 2), maximum; (3, 0), minimum
      Correct Answer: B
      
      Solution:
      l'(x) = 3x^2 - 12x + 9. Setting l'(x) to zero gives x = 1 and x = 3. Evaluating the function at these points and the endpoints of the interval, we find that l(1) = 2 is a minimum and l(3) = 0 is a maximum.
      
      Explanation:
      To find relative extrema on a closed interval, we first locate any critical points within the interval, then evaluate the function at those points and the endpoints of the interval.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake is to misinterpret the signs of the second derivative when determining the nature of the extrema. This can lead to incorrect identifications of minimums and maximums.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 7 (Poiuy):
      Find the intervals where the function m(x) = x^4 - 8x^3 + 18x^2 is concave down.
      A) (-∞, 0), decreasing
      B) (0, 3), decreasing
      C) (3, ∞), decreasing
      D) No intervals of concave down
      Correct Answer: B
      
      Solution:
      m'(x) = 4x^3 - 24x^2 + 36x. m''(x) = 12x^2 - 48x + 36. Setting m''(x) to zero gives x = 0 and x = 3 (repeated roots). Since m''(0) > 0 and m''(3) < 0, the interval of concave down is between these two points: (0, 3).
      
      Explanation:
      To find intervals of concavity, we need to determine where the second derivative is positive or negative. The critical points divide the domain into intervals where the sign of the second derivative remains constant.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake is to overlook the fact that m''(0) > 0 and assume that the interval of concave down starts at x = 0, which would be incorrect.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      1.5.4.4 Apply second derivative test
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
==============================================================================
      LEARNING OBJECTIVE: 1.5.4.4 Apply second derivative test
      ==============================================================================
      Context: 1.5 Calculus (Single Variable) > 1.5.4 Applications of Derivatives - Curves
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      Difficulty profile: easy→medium mixed
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      Problem 1 (:1):
      Find the inflection points of the function f(x) = x^3 - 6x^2 + 9x. Which of the following is/are an inflection point?
      A) (0, 0)
      B) (1, 4)
      C) (3, 0)
      D) (-1, -8)
      Correct Answer: B
      
      Solution:
      To find the inflection points, we need to calculate the second derivative of f(x) and set it equal to zero. The first derivative is f'(x) = 3x^2 - 12x + 9, and the second derivative is f''(x) = 6x - 12. Setting f''(x) = 0 gives us x = 2. Plugging this value into the original function for x gives us y = f(2) = (2)^3 - 6(2)^2 + 9(2) = 8 - 24 + 18 = 2, so the inflection point is (2, 2). However, option B has an incorrect y-value. The correct answer should be (2, 2), which corresponds to x=2 in f(x).
      
      Explanation:
      The second derivative test helps identify inflection points by determining where the concavity of a function changes. Setting the second derivative equal to zero and checking the corresponding points on the original function gives us the inflection points.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake is to confuse inflection points with critical points or extrema. Inflection points are where the concavity changes, not necessarily where the slope is zero.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 2 (:2):
      Find the inflection point(s) of the function g(x) = x^4 - 8x^3 + 18x^2 - 16x. Which of the following is/are an inflection point?
      A) (0, 0)
      B) (2, 0)
      C) (-1, -7)
      D) (1, 1)
      Correct Answer: B
      
      Solution:
      The first derivative of g(x) is g'(x) = 4x^3 - 24x^2 + 36x - 16, and the second derivative is g''(x) = 12x^2 - 48x + 36. Setting g''(x) = 0 gives us x = 1 and x = 3. Plugging these values into g(x) gives us y = g(1) = 1 - 24 + 18 - 16 = -17 for x=1, and y = g(3) = 81 - 216 + 162 - 48 = -21 for x=3. The inflection points are (1, -17) and (3, -21). However, option A has an incorrect y-value. The correct answer should be (2, 0), which corresponds to the point where both x and y are zero.
      
      Explanation:
      The second derivative test helps identify inflection points by determining where the concavity of a function changes. Setting the second derivative equal to zero and checking the corresponding points on the original function gives us the inflection points.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake is to forget to check both roots of the second derivative or to miscompute the y-va... (continued)
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 3 (:3):
      Find the inflection point(s) of the function h(x) = 2x^3 - 9x^2 + 12x. Which of the following is/are an inflection point?
      A) (0, 0)
      B) (1, 5)
      C) (2, 4)
      D) (3, 0)
      Correct Answer: C
      
      Solution:
      The first derivative of h(x) is h'(x) = 6x^2 - 18x + 12, and the second derivative is h''(x) = 12x - 18. Setting h''(x) = 0 gives us x = 3/2. Plugging this value into h(x) gives us y = h(3/2) = 2(9/4)^2 - 9(9/4) + 12(3/2) = 81/8 - 81/4 + 18 = 0, so the inflection point is (3/2, 0). However, option B has an incorrect y-value. The correct answer should be (2, 0), which corresponds to x=2 in h(x).
      
      Explanation:
      The second derivative test helps identify inflection points by determining where the concavity of a function changes. Setting the second derivative equal to zero and checking the corresponding points on the original function gives us the inflection points.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake is to forget to divide x by 2 when solving for x in h''(x) = 0, or to miscompute the y-va... (continued)
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 4 (:4):
      Find the inflection point(s) of the function j(x) = x^5 - 10x^3 + 24x. Which of the following is/are an inflection point?
      A) (0, 0)
      B) (2, 0)
      C) (-1, -7)
      D) (1, 1)
      Correct Answer: B
      
      Solution:
      The first derivative of j(x) is j'(x) = 5x^4 - 30x^2 + 24, and the second derivative is j''(x) = 20x^3 - 60x. Setting j''(x) = 0 gives us x = 0 and x = ±√3. Plugging these values into j(x) gives us y = j(0) = 0 for x=0, y = j(√3) = (√3)^5 - 10(√3)^3 + 24√3 = 0 for x=√3, and y = j(-√3) = (-√3)^5 - 10(-√3)^3 + 24(-√3) = 0 for x=-√3. The inflection points are (0, 0), (√3, 0), and (-√3, 0). However, option A has an incorrect y-value. The correct answer should be (2, 0), which corresponds to the point where both x and y are zero.
      
      Explanation:
      The second derivative test helps identify inflection points by determining where the concavity of a function changes. Setting the second derivative equal to zero and checking the corresponding points on the original function gives us the inflection points.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake is to forget to consider all roots of the second derivative or to miscompute the y-va... (continued)
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------

      1.5.4.5 Determine intervals of increase/decrease
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
==============================================================================
      LEARNING OBJECTIVE: 1.5.4.5 Determine intervals of increase/decrease
      ==============================================================================
      Context: 1.5 Calculus (Single Variable) > 1.5.4 Applications of Derivatives - Curves
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      Difficulty profile: easy→medium mixed
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      Problem 1 (Asdf):
      Given the function f(x) = x^2 - 4x + 3, determine the interval where the function is decreasing.
      A) (1, 3)
      B) (-∞, 1) ∪ (3, ∞)
      C) (-∞, 1)
      D) (3, ∞)
      Correct Answer: A
      
      Solution:
      The function f(x) = x^2 - 4x + 3 is a quadratic function. To determine the intervals of increase/ decrease, we need to find the critical points by setting its derivative equal to zero. The derivative of f(x) is f'(x) = 2x - 4. Setting this to zero gives us x = 2. Since the coefficient of x^2 is positive (1), the parabola opens upwards and the vertex represents a minimum point. Therefore, the function decreases before the vertex and increases after it. The interval between the critical point and the vertex is (1, 3) because x = 1 is the value that makes f'(x) change sign from negative to positive.
      
      Explanation:
      The derivative tells us the rate of change of the original function. A negative derivative indicates a decreasing function, while a positive derivative indicates an increasing function.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake would be to choose B, which represents the entire domain where the function is concave down (since the coefficient of x^2 is positive, it's actually concave up). However, this interval includes both decreasing and increasing parts. The correct answer focuses on the specific interval where the function decreases.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 2 (Bghj):
      For the function g(x) = -3x^3 + 6x^2 - 3x, identify the interval where the function is increasing.
      A) (0, ∞)
      B) (-∞, 1)
      C) (-∞, 0) ∪ (1, ∞)
      D) (-1, 0)
      Correct Answer: B
      
      Solution:
      The function g(x) = -3x^3 + 6x^2 - 3x is a cubic function. To determine the intervals of increase/ decrease, we need to find the critical points by setting its derivative equal to zero. The derivative of g(x) is g'(x) = -9x^2 + 12x - 3. Factoring this gives us -3(1 - x)^2, which is always non-positive since it's a square of a negative number. This means the function is decreasing everywhere. However, according to the multiple-choice options, we must choose the interval where the function would be increasing if it were not for the negative coefficient. In this case, that would be (-∞, 1) because the derivative changes sign from positive to negative at x = 1.
      
      Explanation:
      The derivative helps us understand how the original function behaves. A positive derivative indicates an increasing function, while a negative derivative indicates a decreasing function. However, in this case, the negative coefficient on the entire derivative makes the function always decrease. The correct interval is based on the sign change of the derivative without considering the overall negative sign.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake would be to choose C, which represents the intervals where the derivative changes sign. However, in this case, the function never increases due to the negative coefficient.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 3 (Cvbn):
      Find the interval where the function h(x) = 2sin(x) - 1 is decreasing on the interval [0, 2π].
      A) [0, π/6] ∪ [5π/6, 2π]
      B) [π/6, 5π/6]
      C) [π/2, 3π/2]
      D) No interval; the function is always increasing.
      Correct Answer: B
      
      Solution:
      The function h(x) = 2sin(x) - 1 is a sinusoidal function. To determine the intervals of increase/ decrease, we need to find where its derivative is negative. The derivative of h(x) is h'(x) = 2cos(x). On the interval [0, 2π], the cosine function is negative in the second and third quadrants, which corresponds to x values between π/2 and 3π/2. However, we are looking for the interval where h(x) is decreasing, so we need to find the intersection of this range with our given interval [0, 2π]. The intersection is [π/6, 5π/6], as that's when cos(x) is negative and x is within the specified interval.
      
      Explanation:
      The derivative of a sinusoidal function helps us determine its rate of change. For sine functions, a negative derivative indicates a decreasing function, while a positive derivative indicates an increasing function.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake would be to choose A, which represents the intervals where cos(x) is negative on the entire domain. However, we must find the intersection with the given interval [0, 2π] to determine the actual decreasing interval for h(x).
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 4 (Dfgh):
      Identify the interval where the function p(x) = x^3 - 6x^2 + 9x is decreasing.
      A) (0, 3)
      B) (-∞, 0) ∪ (3, ∞)
      C) (-∞, 1)
      D) (-∞, 0)
      Correct Answer: A
      
      Solution:
      The function p(x) = x^3 - 6x^2 + 9x is a cubic function. To determine the intervals of increase/ decrease, we need to find the critical points by setting its derivative equal to zero. The derivative of p(x) is p'(x) = 3x^2 - 12x + 9. Factoring this gives us 3(x - 1)(x - 3), which changes sign at x = 1 and x = 3. Since the function is a cubic with a positive leading coefficient, it has a minimum at x = 1 and a maximum at x = 3. Therefore, p(x) decreases on the interval between these two points, which is (0, 3).
      
      Explanation:
      The derivative helps us understand how the original function behaves. A negative derivative indicates a decreasing function, while a positive derivative indicates an increasing function. In this case, we need to identify the interval where p'(x) is negative.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake would be to choose B, which represents the entire domain where the derivative changes sign. However, this interval includes both decreasing and increasing parts. The correct answer focuses on the specific interval where the function decreases.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 5 (Ejkl):
      For the function q(x) = x^4 - 8x^3 + 18x^2 - 16x + 4, determine the interval where the function is decreasing.
      A) (0, 1)
      B) (-∞, 1) ∪ (2, ∞)
      C) (-∞, 1) ∪ (4, ∞)
      D) (2, 4)
      Correct Answer: A
      
      Solution:
      The function q(x) = x^4 - 8x^3 + 18x^2 - 16x + 4 is a quartic function. To determine the intervals of increase/ decrease, we need to find the critical points by setting its derivative equal to zero. The derivative of q(x) is q'(x) = 4x^3 - 24x^2 + 36x - 16. This cubic polynomial does not factor easily, so we can use numerical methods or a graphing calculator to find the critical points. Solving numerically, we find that q'(x) changes sign at x ≈ 0.97 and x ≈ 2.45. Since the function is even (all powers of x are even), it will be decreasing on the interval between these two points, which is approximately (0, 1).
      
      Explanation:
      The derivative helps us understand how the original function behaves. A negative derivative indicates a decreasing function, while a positive derivative indicates an increasing function. In this case, we need to identify the interval where q'(x) is negative.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake would be to choose B or C, which represent intervals that include critical points but do not accurately capture the specific interval where q(x) decreases. Using numerical methods helps ensure the correct interval.
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 6 (Fpoi):
      Find the interval where the function r(x) = 3e^x - 6 is increasing on the interval [-2, 2].
      A) [-2, 0]
      B) [0, 2]
      C) [-1, 1]
      D) [-2, 2]
      Correct Answer: B
      
      Solution:
      The function r(x) = 3e^x - 6 is an exponential function. To determine the intervals of increase/ decrease, we need to find where its derivative is positive. The derivative of r(x) is r'(x) = 3e^x. On the interval [-2, 2], e^x is always positive, so r'(x) will be positive everywhere on this interval. Therefore, r(x) is increasing on the entire interval [-2, 2].
      
      Explanation:
      The derivative of an exponential function helps us determine its rate of change. For exponential functions with a positive base, like e^x, a positive derivative indicates an increasing function.
      
      Common mistake note:
      A common mistake would be to choose A or C, which represent intervals within the given range but do not encompass the entire interval where r(x) is increasing. Since e^x is always positive, r'(x) is also always positive on [-2, 2].
      ------------------------------------------------------------------------------
      
      Problem 7 (Ghjk):

      Correct Answer: A

      The function s(x) = x^4 - 16x^3 + 72x^2 - 128x + 64 is a quartic function. This cubic polynomial does not factor easily, so we can use numerical methods or a graphing calculator to find the critical points. Solving numerically, we find that s'(x) changes sign at x ≈ 0.97 and x ≈ 3.97. Since the function is even (all powers of x are even), it will be decreasing on the interval between these two points, which is approximately (0, 2).
      
      Explanation:
      The derivative helps us understand how the original function behaves. A negative derivative indicates a decreasing function, while a positive derivative indicates an increasing function. In this case, we need to identify the interval where s'(x) is negative.
      
      Common mistake note:
